CONSTITUTIONAL LAW 1

Complete ALAC Case Digest Compendium • 149 Landmark Supreme Court Decisions

★ COMPENDIUM OVERVIEW & METHODOLOGY This master compendium contains comprehensive case digests for all 149 landmark Philippine Supreme Court decisions assigned in Constitutional Law 1, fully structured under the [ALAC] Method (Answer, Legal Basis, Application/Analysis, Conclusion/Dispositive Ruling). Each case digest concludes with Section IV specifying the precise Applicable Constitutional Articles and Sections (1987 Philippine Constitution) accompanied by an in-depth doctrinal synthesis linking the ruling directly to the core principles and examination syllabus. Organized systematically across Topics I through VII: The Philippine Constitution, The State & State Immunity, Fundamental Powers of the State, State Policies & Principles, Legislative Department, Executive Department, and Judicial Department.

TOPIC I: THE PHILIPPINE CONSTITUTION

Amendments, Revision, Ratification, Effectivity, and Judicial Review

★ TOPIC I • KEY CONSTITUTIONAL DOCTRINES & LECTURE OUTLINE • Self-Executing Effectivity: The 1987 Constitution took effect on February 2, 1987, the date of its ratification in the plebiscite (De Leon v. Esguerra). • Amendment vs. Revision: The Two-Part Test (Quantitative and Qualitative Tests) distinguishes piecemeal amendments from structural revisions (Lambino v. COMELEC). • People's Initiative Safeguards: Limited strictly to amendments, requiring a complete enabling statute and direct proposal by the people (Defensor-Santiago & Lambino). • Proper Submission Doctrine: Amendments must be submitted in a unified, non-piecemeal manner allowing enlightened voter choice (Tolentino, Almario, Gonzales).
CASE 1

De Leon vs. Esguerra

CITATION 153 SCRA 602, G.R. No. L-78059, August 31, 1987 | Supreme Court En Banc (Teehankee, C.J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Petitioner Alfredo De Leon was elected Barangay Captain of Dolores, Taytay, Rizal in the May 17, 1982 barangay elections under the 1973 Constitution and BP Blg. 222 (Barangay Election Act of 1982). On February 9, 1987, following the February 2, 1987 plebiscite that ratified the 1987 Constitution, OIC Governor Benjamin Esguerra issued a memorandum designating respondent Florentino G. Magno as replacement Barangay Captain of Dolores. De Leon challenged the designation, asserting that the 1987 Constitution had already taken effect on the date of its ratification in the plebiscite on February 2, 1987, pursuant to Article XVIII, Section 27, thereby superseding the provisional Freedom Constitution and extinguishing the executive's extraordinary power to replace elected local officials.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the 1987 Philippine Constitution took effect on the date of its ratification in the plebiscite on February 2, 1987, or on the date of the official presidential proclamation of the plebiscite results on February 11, 1987.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The 1987 Philippine Constitution took effect on February 2, 1987, the date of its ratification in the national plebiscite, and not on the date the results were proclaimed.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVIII, Section 27 of the 1987 Constitution explicitly commands: 'This Constitution shall take effect immediately upon its ratification by a majority of the votes cast in a plebiscite held for the purpose and shall supersede all previous Constitutions.' Under constitutional interpretation rules, unambiguous express constitutional provisions must be given their plain and literal meaning (verba legis).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court ruled that the record of the Constitutional Commission clearly established that the new charter was intended to take effect on the very day of the plebiscite (February 2, 1987), when the sovereign people cast their votes in favor of the new fundamental law. The subsequent proclamation by President Corazon C. Aquino on February 11, 1987 (Proclamation No. 58) was merely an administrative and declaratory confirmation of a fait accompli. Consequently, as of February 2, 1987, the provisional Freedom Constitution (Proclamation No. 3) ceased to exist. The power of the OIC Governor to designate replacements for elected local officials under the provisional charter was automatically terminated. Under Article XVIII, Section 8, incumbent barangay officials were entitled to continue in office until otherwise provided by law.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court declared the designation of respondent Magno NULL and VOID, and permanently enjoined respondents from interfering with petitioner De Leon's lawful assumption and discharge of duties as Barangay Captain.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVIII, Section 27 (Effectivity of Constitution) & Article XVIII, Section 8 (Transitory Term of Barangay Officials) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: This landmark case establishes the foundational rule on the effectivity of the 1987 Constitution. Unlike ordinary statutes that require publication before effectivity under Article 2 of the Civil Code, a Constitution is self-executing and takes effect on the precise date of popular ratification when explicitly stipulated by its own transitory provisions. It marks the instantaneous transition from revolutionary governance back to constitutional democracy.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 2

Lambino vs. Commission on Elections

CITATION 505 SCRA 160, G.R. No. 174153, October 25, 2006 | Supreme Court En Banc (Carpio, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Lambino Group gathered signatures of 6.3 million registered voters to propose amendments to the 1987 Constitution through a People's Initiative under Article XVII, Section 2. The proposal sought to transform the Philippine presidential bicameral system into a unicameral parliamentary form of government by modifying Articles VI and VII and inserting a new transitory article. The Lambino Group petitioned the Commission on Elections (COMELEC) to hold a plebiscite. COMELEC denied the petition on the ground that Republic Act No. 6735 had been declared inadequate in Defensor-Santiago v. COMELEC and that the proposal constituted a revision rather than a mere amendment. Petitioners elevated the matter via petition for certiorari to the Supreme Court.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether the Lambino Group's initiative petition complies with the constitutional requirement that the initiative must be directly proposed by the people. 2. Whether the proposed shift from a Presidential Bicameral system to a Parliamentary Unicameral system constitutes an 'amendment' or a 'revision' under Article XVII.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO to both. The petition failed to show that the signers saw the full text of the proposed changes, and the shift from presidential to parliamentary system is a radical revision, which cannot be undertaken via People's Initiative.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVII, Section 2 of the 1987 Constitution limits People's Initiative exclusively to 'amendments' and mandates that it be 'directly proposed by the people through initiative upon a petition.' Revision involves a re-examination, overhaul, or alteration of the basic principles and structure of the Constitution, which can only be proposed by Congress as a Constituent Assembly (Sec. 1[1]) or a Constitutional Convention (Sec. 1[2]).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court formulated the definitive 'Two-Part Test' to distinguish an Amendment from a Revision: 1. Quantitative Test: Focuses on the number of provisions altered, examined, or affected. 2. Qualitative Test: Inquires whether the proposed change alters the fundamental structure, system of checks and balances, or basic underlying principles of government. Here, shifting from presidential bicameralism to parliamentary unicameralism profoundly overhauls the structure of government, merges executive and legislative branches, and abolishes the Senate, thus failing the qualitative test and constituting a revision. Furthermore, petitioners presented only a signature sheet without attaching the full text of the draft charter to the voters when gathering signatures, violating the fundamental requirement of direct popular authorship.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition and AFFIRMED the COMELEC Resolution rejecting the plebiscite petition for People's Initiative.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVII, Section 2 (Amendments via People's Initiative) & Article XVII, Section 1 (Revision via Constituent Assembly/Con-Con) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Lambino v. COMELEC is the leading modern Philippine authority establishing the Quantitative and Qualitative Tests for constitutional change. It establishes the inviolable doctrine that the sovereign people reserve the power of initiative only for piecemeal amendments, while structural, foundational overhauls (revisions) remain strictly confined to representative assemblies (Congress as Con-Ass or Constitutional Conventions).

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 3

Imbong vs. Commission on Elections

CITATION 35 SCRA 28, G.R. No. L-32432, September 11, 1970 | Supreme Court En Banc (Makasiar, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Petitioners challenged the constitutionality of Republic Act No. 6132 (The 1971 Constitutional Convention Act), which implemented Resolution of Both Houses (RBH) No. 2 and RBH No. 4 calling for a Constitutional Convention to propose revisions to the 1935 Constitution. Specifically, petitioners questioned Section 8(a) of RA 6132, which prohibited political parties and organizations from nominating or aiding candidates for delegates to the Constitutional Convention, as well as the authority of Congress to pass an implementing statute regulating the details of the convention.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether Congress, acting as a legislative body, has the authority to enact an implementing statute (RA 6132) providing for the composition, qualifications, election, and regulation of delegates to a Constitutional Convention.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Congress possesses the plenary power to enact enabling legislation to implement its constituent resolution calling for a Constitutional Convention.

LEGAL BASIS Article XV, Section 1 of the 1935 Constitution (analogous to Article XVII, Section 3 of the 1987 Constitution). The constituent power to call a Constitutional Convention includes all subsidiary, necessary, and incidental legislative powers to provide for the mechanics, apportionment of seats, qualifications, conduct of elections, and non-partisan character of the convention.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Court ruled that while Congress acts as a Constituent Assembly when deciding to call a Constitutional Convention by a two-thirds vote, it exercises its regular legislative powers when passing the implementing details (such as appropriations, election schedules, and delegate qualifications). The ban on partisan political intervention was a valid exercise of police power and legislative authority designed to insulate constitutional delegates from partisan control and preserve the integrity and independence of the constituent process.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court UPHELD the constitutionality of RA 6132 and DISMISSED the petitions.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVII, Section 3 (Call for Constitutional Convention) & Article VI, Section 1 (Plenary Legislative Power) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Imbong highlights the distinct dual capacities of Congress: (1) acting as a Constituent Assembly to initiate constitutional change, and (2) acting in its regular legislative capacity to enact statutory frameworks providing funding, administrative machinery, and regulatory standards for constitutional conventions.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 4

Defensor-Santiago vs. Commission on Elections

CITATION 270 SCRA 106, G.R. No. 127325, March 19, 1997 | Supreme Court En Banc (Davide, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Private respondent Movement for People's Initiative for Reform, Modernization, and Action (PIRMA) launched a signature campaign to amend the 1987 Constitution by lifting term limits of elected officials, including the President. PIRMA filed a petition with COMELEC invoking Republic Act No. 6735 (The Initiative and Referendum Act) and COMELEC Resolution No. 2300. Senator Miriam Defensor-Santiago and others filed a petition for prohibition before the Supreme Court, arguing that RA 6735 was incomplete and inadequate to govern people's initiative on the Constitution.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether Republic Act No. 6735 is an adequate and sufficient enabling law to implement the constitutional system of People's Initiative on constitutional amendments under Article XVII, Section 2.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. RA 6735 is incomplete, inadequate, and insufficient to implement the system of People's Initiative to amend the Constitution.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVII, Section 2 of the 1987 Constitution states that amendments may be proposed directly by the people 'as may be provided by law.' Under the Completeness and Sufficient Standard Tests of legislative delegation, an enabling law must be complete in all its terms so that nothing is left to the judgment of the administrative agency.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS A meticulous review of RA 6735 revealed that while the statute comprehensively provided for initiatives on national laws and local ordinances, it neglected to provide a detailed statutory procedure for constitutional initiatives. The subtitle on national initiative mentions only statutes, and the law fails to provide for the crucial requirements of the petition's contents, verification of signatures, and threshold safeguards. COMELEC Resolution No. 2300 could not supply the statutory omission because administrative rules cannot supplement or expand an incomplete statute.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, declared RA 6735 inadequate for constitutional amendments, declared COMELEC Resolution No. 2300 void in that respect, and permanently ENJOINED COMELEC from entertaining PIRMA's petition.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVII, Section 2 (System of Initiative on Constitutional Amendments) & Article VI, Section 1 (Legislative Power & Non-Delegation) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Defensor-Santiago firmly established that Article XVII, Section 2 is not self-executing and strictly requires a comprehensive congressional enabling act. It underscored that without a complete legislative framework passed by Congress, no constitutional amendment via People's Initiative can lawfully proceed.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 5

Lambino vs. Commission on Elections (Resolution on Motion for Reconsideration)

CITATION G.R. No. 174153, November 21, 2006 | Supreme Court En Banc (Carpio, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following the promulgation of the October 25, 2006 decision in Lambino v. COMELEC, petitioners filed a Motion for Reconsideration, arguing among others that RA 6735 should be declared sufficient, that the Defensor-Santiago ruling was a mere obiter dictum on the insufficiency of RA 6735, and that the people's sovereign will should not be thwarted by procedural technicalities.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the Motion for Reconsideration should be granted and whether Defensor-Santiago's declaration of RA 6735's insufficiency should be overturned.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. The Supreme Court DENIED the Motion for Reconsideration with finality.

LEGAL BASIS The Doctrine of Stare Decisis et non quieta movere (adhere to precedents and do not unsettle things which are established) and Article XVII, Section 2 of the Constitution.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Court held that ten Justices reaffirmed the October 25, 2006 ruling that the proposed change was a revision rather than an amendment. Even assuming arguendo that RA 6735 was adequate, the petition remained fatally defective on two substantive constitutional grounds: (1) the proposal constituted an unconstitutional revision beyond the scope of People's Initiative, and (2) the signatures gathered failed the direct proposal requirement because the signers were not provided the full text of the proposed changes.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DENIED the Motion for Reconsideration with FINALITY.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVII, Section 2 (Scope of People's Initiative) & Article XVII, Section 1 (Constitutional Revision) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: The Lambino MR resolution solidified the dual-barrier doctrine: even if statutory authorization were present, the substantive boundary between an amendment and a revision remains an insurmountable constitutional barrier against restructuring government via People's Initiative.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 6

Gonzales vs. Commission on Elections

CITATION 21 SCRA 774, G.R. No. L-28196, November 9, 1967 | Supreme Court En Banc (Concepcion, C.J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

In 1967, Congress passed RBH No. 1 (increasing the membership of the House of Representatives) and RBH No. 3 (allowing members of Congress to run for Constitutional Convention delegates without forfeiting their seats), and enacted Republic Act No. 4913 submitting these proposed amendments for ratification in the upcoming November 1967 regular general elections. Ramon A. Gonzales filed a petition for prohibition, asserting that: (1) Congress cannot propose constitutional amendments without an express grant of constituent power; and (2) constitutional amendments cannot be submitted for ratification during a regular general election.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether the power of Congress to propose constitutional amendments is subject to judicial review. 2. Whether proposed constitutional amendments may be submitted for ratification simultaneously with regular general elections.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER 1. YES, constituent acts of Congress are subject to judicial review. 2. YES, submitting proposed amendments during a regular general election is constitutionally permissible.

LEGAL BASIS Article XV, Section 1 of the 1935 Constitution (analogous to Article XVII, Section 4 of the 1987 Constitution). The Constitution requires that amendments be submitted 'in an election' or 'plebiscite' called for that purpose, which does not preclude holding it synchronously with a regular general election.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the exercise of constituent power is not a purely political question immune from judicial scrutiny; the Court has the jurisdiction to determine whether constituent bodies complied with constitutional requirements. On the substantive issue, the Court held that the term 'plebiscite' or 'election' includes general elections, provided that the amendments are clearly submitted to the people so that their will can be intelligently expressed. Holding the plebiscite on the same day as the regular elections saves substantial public funds and does not vitiate voter consent.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition and SUSTAINED the validity of RA 4913.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVII, Section 4 (Ratification Plebiscite) & Article VIII, Section 1 (Judicial Power to Review Constituent Actions) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Gonzales v. COMELEC established two bedrock doctrines: (1) Constituent power is a delegated power governed by the Constitution and subject to judicial review; and (2) Synchronized submission of constitutional amendments during general elections is constitutionally valid provided proper submission standards are observed.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 7

Occeña vs. Commission on Elections

CITATION 104 SCRA 1, G.R. No. L-56350, April 2, 1981 | Supreme Court En Banc (Fernando, C.J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Petitioners Samuel Occeña and Ramon A. Gonzales challenged the validity of Resolution Nos. 1, 2, and 3 of the Interim Batasang Pambansa proposing constitutional amendments to the 1973 Constitution (creating an Executive Committee, modifying executive tenure, and institutionalizing parliamentary mechanics). Petitioners argued that the Interim Batasang Pambansa lacked constituent authority to revise the Constitution and that the three-week period provided between the approval of the resolutions and the April 7, 1981 plebiscite was grossly inadequate for the electorate to study the proposals.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the Interim Batasang Pambansa possesses constituent power to propose amendments to the Constitution, and whether the time frame provided before the plebiscite satisfied the requirement of fair and intelligent submission.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The Interim Batasang Pambansa had full constituent authority under the 1976 Amendments, and the time allotted was legally adequate.

LEGAL BASIS The 1976 Amendments to the 1973 Constitution expressly vested the Interim Batasang Pambansa with the same constituent powers as the regular National Assembly to propose constitutional amendments.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Court ruled that the Interim Batasang Pambansa was explicitly clothed with constituent power by the sovereign will expressed in the 1976 plebiscite. Regarding the sufficiency of time, the Court noted that extensive public debates, media broadcasts, and community assemblies had taken place, and the proposed changes were widely published and debated across the nation. The period was deemed sufficient for an informed sovereign choice.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petitions and UPHELD the validity of the resolutions and the plebiscite.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVII, Section 1 (Constituent Authority of Legislative Body) & Article XVII, Section 4 (Sufficiency of Plebiscite Notice) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Occeña reinforces the principle that constituent bodies possess broad discretion in determining the timing of plebiscites, provided the electorate is afforded reasonable access to information and meaningful public debate to exercise their sovereign voting prerogative.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 8

Almario vs. Alba

CITATION 127 SCRA 69, G.R. No. L-66088, January 25, 1984 | Supreme Court En Banc (Gutierrez, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Petitioners challenged the constitutionality of the January 27, 1984 plebiscite on amendments to the 1973 Constitution (covering presidential succession, creation of the Office of the Vice-President, and granting public land grants). Petitioners contended that the electorate was not given sufficient time to understand the complex proposals and that the ballot questions were framed in a manner that violated the Doctrine of Proper Submission.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the proposed amendments were submitted to the people with sufficient time and clarity to satisfy the Doctrine of Proper Submission.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The submission satisfied constitutional standards because the proposals were widely disseminated, the ballot questions were clear, and the electorate was provided reasonable opportunity to decide.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Sections 1 and 2 of the 1973 Constitution (analogous to Article XVII, Section 4 of the 1987 Constitution). The Doctrine of Proper Submission requires that proposed amendments be submitted to the people under conditions that enable them to intelligently understand and vote on the specific changes.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the Constitution does not fix a rigid statutory duration between the passage of constituent resolutions and the plebiscite date. As long as there is widespread publication, public discussions, and no deliberate concealment, the requirement of intelligent sovereign choice is fulfilled. Furthermore, presenting multiple distinct questions separately on the ballot allows voters to accept some amendments and reject others, thereby preserving genuine freedom of choice.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition and PERMITTED the holding of the January 27, 1984 plebiscite.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVII, Section 4 (Proper Submission & Ratification Mechanics) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Almario v. Alba emphasizes that the cornerstone of the Doctrine of Proper Submission is voter enlightenment and freedom of choice. Voters must be given clear, uncoerced alternatives and adequate opportunity to understand the ramifications of each proposed constitutional modification.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 9

Tolentino vs. Commission on Elections

CITATION 41 SCRA 702, G.R. No. L-34150, October 16, 1971 | Supreme Court En Banc (Barredo, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The 1971 Constitutional Convention approved 'Organic Resolution No. 1' proposing to lower the voting age from 21 to 18 years, and resolved to submit this single amendment to the electorate in a plebiscite on November 8, 1971, ahead of the completion and submission of the rest of the new constitutional draft. Arturo M. Tolentino filed a petition for prohibition to restrain COMELEC from holding the plebiscite, arguing that a Constitutional Convention cannot submit piecemeal amendments for ratification.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether a Constitutional Convention can validly submit a single, isolated amendment to the electorate for ratification while continuing its work on revising the rest of the Constitution (Piecemeal Ratification).

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Constitutional amendments or revisions proposed by a Constitutional Convention must be submitted to the people in a single, comprehensive election/plebiscite, and not piecemeal.

LEGAL BASIS Article XV, Section 1 of the 1935 Constitution (and Article XVII, Section 4 of the 1987 Constitution). The singular word 'an election' signifies that all proposals must be presented together to enable the electorate to evaluate the entire constitutional framework as an integrated whole.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court articulated the classic 'Doctrine of Proper Submission.' The Court ruled that when a Constitutional Convention undertakes to rewrite the fundamental law, it cannot present isolated parts for premature ratification because constitutional provisions are interdependent. The people cannot intelligently judge a single provision without knowing its interplay with the rest of the emerging charter. A piecemeal submission deprives the sovereign electorate of the complete context necessary for an enlightened decision.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, declared Organic Resolution No. 1 and its implementing resolutions UNCONSTITUTIONAL and VOID, and ENJOINED the scheduled plebiscite.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVII, Section 4 (Doctrine of Proper Submission & Holistic Ratification) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Tolentino v. COMELEC is the quintessential Philippine precedent barring piecemeal constitutional submission. It guarantees that the sovereign people are never forced to ratify isolated constitutional fragments without having the entire unified constitutional framework before them.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 10

Javellana vs. The Executive Secretary

CITATION 50 SCRA 30, G.R. No. L-36142, March 31, 1973 | Supreme Court En Banc (Concepcion, C.J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following the declaration of Martial Law under Proclamation No. 1081 in September 1972, the 1971 Constitutional Convention completed the draft 1973 Constitution. President Ferdinand E. Marcos issued Presidential Decree No. 86 organizing 'Citizens Assemblies' (Barangays). Instead of holding a secret ballot plebiscite under the Election Code, the draft Constitution was presented in Citizens Assemblies via show of hands. On January 17, 1973, the President issued Proclamation No. 1102 certifying that the 1973 Constitution had been ratified by an overwhelming majority. Josue Javellana and others challenged Proclamation No. 1102, asserting that the viva voce voting in Citizens Assemblies was not the plebiscite mandated by the Constitution.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether the validity of the ratification of the 1973 Constitution is a justiciable constitutional issue or an unreviewable political question. 2. Whether the 1973 Constitution was validly ratified in accordance with the 1935 Constitution. 3. Whether the 1973 Constitution is in force and effect.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER 1. It is a JUSTICIABLE issue subject to judicial review. 2. NO, it was NOT validly ratified in accordance with Article XV of the 1935 Constitution. 3. YES, there are no further judicial obstacles to the 1973 Constitution being considered in force and effect due to popular acquiescence and political reality.

LEGAL BASIS Article XV, Section 1 of the 1935 Constitution required a formal plebiscite with secret ballots administered by the Commission on Elections. Article VIII, Section 1 of the Judicial Power Clause guarantees judicial review over constitutional compliance.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS A majority of six Justices held that the issue of ratification was justiciable and not a political question. A majority of six Justices also explicitly held that the 1973 Constitution was NOT validly ratified because voice votes and show of hands in informal Citizens Assemblies did not comply with the mandatory secret ballot plebiscite required by law. However, on the ultimate question of whether the charter was in force, four Justices held it was in force, four held it was not, and two dismissed the petition on political question grounds. With no majority of eight votes required to declare Proclamation No. 1102 invalid, Chief Justice Concepcion penned the historic dispositive conclusion.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court concluded: 'There is no further judicial obstacle to the new Constitution being considered in force and effect.' Petitions were DISMISSED.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVII, Section 4 (Mandatory Formal Plebiscite) & Article VIII, Section 1 (Judicial Power over Political Question Doctrine) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: The Javellana Ratification Cases stand as one of the most historically significant rulings in Philippine constitutional law. It established that the process of constitutional ratification is strictly justiciable, but illustrated the acute tension between strict constitutional legality and pragmatic judicial acquiescence under revolutionary/de facto political circumstances.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

TOPIC II: THE PHILIPPINES AS A STATE & STATE IMMUNITY

National Territory, Sovereignty, State Immunity, and Exceptions

★ TOPIC II • KEY CONSTITUTIONAL DOCTRINES & LECTURE OUTLINE • Restrictive Theory of Sovereign Immunity: Distinguishes acts jure imperii (sovereign/governmental, immune) from acts jure gestionis (proprietary/commercial, not immune) (The Holy See, USA v. Ruiz, CNMEG). • Functional Immunity of International Organizations: Functional immunity protects official institutional acts, never criminal offenses or torts (Liang v. People, SEAFDEC, Callado v. IRRI). • Suability vs. Liability: Consent to be sued opens the courthouse door but does not concede substantive liability (Meritt v. Government of the Philippine Islands). • Non-Garnishment of Public Funds: Public funds cannot be garnished without a legislative appropriation ordinance; money claims must be submitted to the Commission on Audit (UP v. Dizon, San Miguel, Makati v. CA).
CASE 11

The Holy See vs. Rosario, Jr.

CITATION 238 SCRA 524, G.R. No. 101949, December 1, 1994 | Supreme Court En Banc (Quiason, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Holy See owned a parcel of land in Parañaque City (Lot 5-A) which it authorized Papal Nuncio Msgr. Torpigliani to sell. Star Group Resources, Inc. negotiated to purchase the lot for commercial development. However, squatters refused to vacate the premises, causing the deal to stall. The Holy See then sold the property to Tropicana Properties Corporation. Star Group filed a complaint for annulment of sale, specific performance, and damages against the Holy See before the RTC of Makati. The Holy See filed a motion to dismiss invoking sovereign state immunity. The RTC denied the motion, and the Department of Foreign Affairs (DFA) intervened confirming that the Holy See enjoyed diplomatic immunity as an international sovereign entity.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the Holy See can claim sovereign state immunity from suit in Philippine courts regarding the sale of real property located in the Philippines.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The Holy See is immune from suit because the acquisition and sale of the lot was an act jure imperii (governmental/sovereign in character) and not an act jure gestionis (purely commercial).

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Section 3 of the 1987 Constitution ('The State may not be sued without its consent') and the Restrictive Doctrine of Sovereign Immunity under Public International Law. Under the restrictive theory, immunity is recognized only with respect to acts jure imperii (sovereign acts) and denied with respect to acts jure gestionis (commercial/proprietary acts).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the Holy See is recognized in international law as an international person and sovereign entity. In determining whether an act is jure imperii or jure gestionis, the test is the nature and purpose of the act. The Holy See acquired the lot to construct the Papal Nunciature (official diplomatic residence). When the surrounding area became unsuitable due to squatters, the Holy See decided to dispose of the property. The sale was not conducted for commercial profit or business gain, but to relocate the diplomatic mission. Furthermore, the official certification by the Department of Foreign Affairs asserting the diplomatic status of the Holy See is a political determination binding on domestic courts.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, REVERSED the RTC orders, and ORDERED the dismissal of the civil complaint against the Holy See.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (State Immunity from Suit) & Article II, Section 2 (Incorporation Clause of Public International Law) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: The Holy See v. Rosario is the leading authority on the Restrictive Theory of Sovereign Immunity and the distinction between acts jure imperii and acts jure gestionis. It establishes that sovereign states retain absolute immunity for governmental and diplomatic acts, and domestic courts must defer to the executive's diplomatic determinations.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 12

Liang (Huefeng) vs. People

CITATION 323 SCRA 652, G.R. No. 125865, January 28, 2000 | Supreme Court First Division (Ynares-Santiago, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Petitioner Jeffrey Liang, an economist employed by the Asian Development Bank (ADB), was charged before the Metropolitan Trial Court (MeTC) of Mandaluyong with two counts of Grave Oral Defamation for uttering allegedly slanderous, malicious, and defamatory words against his fellow ADB worker, Joyce Cabal. Liang claimed diplomatic immunity under the ADB Headquarters Agreement and secured an endorsement from the Department of Foreign Affairs requesting dismissal. The MeTC dismissed the cases, but the RTC reversed and ordered the trial to proceed.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether international agency officials (such as ADB personnel) enjoy blanket immunity from criminal prosecution for slanderous or defamatory statements.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. International officials enjoy functional immunity only for acts performed in their official capacity; uttering slanderous remarks is not an official act.

LEGAL BASIS Section 45(a) of the ADB Headquarters Agreement grants immunity to ADB officers and staff only 'in respect of acts performed by them in their official capacity.' Immunity of international officials is functional and strictly limited to legitimate institutional duties.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court ruled that international officials do not possess absolute diplomatic immunity. Their immunity is functional, extending only to acts performed in the official discharge of their institutional duties. Slandering or uttering defamatory words against a colleague can never be part of an economist's official functions for the ADB. A crime or tort cannot be covered by official acts immunity. The court must first conduct a preliminary determination during trial to establish whether the acts were indeed official.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DENIED Liang's petition and AFFIRMED the RTC ruling ordering the trial court to proceed with the criminal prosecution.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (State & Institutional Immunity Limits) & Article II, Section 2 (International Law & Functional Immunity) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Liang v. People defines the vital boundary between absolute sovereign/diplomatic immunity and limited functional immunity of international organization staff. Officials cannot use institutional immunity as an absolute shield to commit crimes, torts, or defamation in the host country.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 13

Arigo vs. Swift

CITATION 735 SCRA 102, G.R. No. 206501, September 16, 2014 | Supreme Court En Banc (Villarama, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

In January 2013, the USS Guardian, a mine countermeasures vessel of the US Navy, ran aground on the South Atoll of Tubbataha Reefs Natural Park, a UNESCO World Heritage Site and marine protected sanctuary in the Sulu Sea, damaging over 2,345 square meters of coral reef. Petitioners, led by Bishop Pedro Arigo and environmental groups, filed a petition for the issuance of a Writ of Kalikasan with TEPO, naming US military commanders (Swift, et al.) and Philippine officials as respondents, demanding environmental damages, rehabilitation funds, and a review of the Visiting Forces Agreement (VFA).

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether US military commanders can be sued in Philippine courts for environmental damage under the Writ of Kalikasan despite sovereign immunity. 2. Whether the VFA waives US sovereign immunity for tortious acts and environmental damage.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER 1. NO. US military officers enjoy sovereign immunity because commanding a naval warship is an act jure imperii. 2. NO. The VFA contains no express waiver of sovereign immunity regarding civil suits in domestic courts.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Section 3 of the 1987 Constitution and the international customary law doctrine of Non-Suability of Foreign States (Par in parem non habet imperium). Under UNCLOS (Articles 30, 31, and 32), warships enjoy sovereign immunity, with the flag state bearing international responsibility via diplomatic channels.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the operation and navigation of a military warship is a quintessential sovereign military function (act jure imperii). A suit against foreign military officers acting in their official military capacities is essentially a suit against the foreign sovereign state itself. In the absence of an express, unequivocal statutory or treaty waiver, Philippine courts have no jurisdiction to award damages against the US government or its naval officers. Redress for coral damage must be pursued through bilateral diplomatic negotiations under international law and UNCLOS, not domestic tort litigation.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition on jurisdictional grounds of state immunity, while noting that the US had already undertaken diplomatic compensation commitments.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Immunity of Foreign States), Article II, Section 2 (Incorporation Clause & UNCLOS), and Article II, Section 16 (Right to Ecology) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Arigo v. Swift confirms the absolute sovereign immunity of foreign naval warships and their commanding officers under both customary international law and UNCLOS. Domestic environmental writs cannot overcome foreign state immunity in the absence of an express treaty waiver.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 14

Southeast Asian Fisheries Development Center (SEAFDEC) vs. NLRC

CITATION 241 SCRA 580, G.R. No. 86773, February 14, 1995 | Supreme Court First Division (Bellosillo, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Private respondent Juvenal Lazaga was employed as a Senior External Affairs Officer at the Aquaculture Department of SEAFDEC in Tigbauan, Iloilo. After his services were terminated, Lazaga filed a complaint for illegal dismissal and separation pay with the National Labor Relations Commission (NLRC). SEAFDEC moved to dismiss the complaint on the ground that it is an international intergovernmental organization established by treaty and enjoys jurisdictional immunity. The Labor Arbiter and NLRC denied the motion and assumed jurisdiction.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether SEAFDEC, as an international intergovernmental organization, is immune from the jurisdiction of the NLRC and Philippine labor courts.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. SEAFDEC enjoys complete immunity from the jurisdiction of local labor tribunals.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Section 3 of the Constitution and the international legal personality of Intergovernmental Organizations established by regional treaty. International organizations enjoy immunity from local jurisdiction to safeguard their independence and prevent unilateral interference by host governments.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that SEAFDEC is an international agency established by treaty among Southeast Asian governments. To allow local labor arbiters and courts to assert jurisdiction over internal personnel disputes of an international organization would infringe upon its institutional autonomy. Unless an international organization expressly waives its immunity, local tribunals are completely deprived of subject-matter jurisdiction.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, SET ASIDE the NLRC resolutions, and DISMISSED the labor complaint for lack of jurisdiction.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (State & Institutional Immunity) & Article II, Section 2 (Treaty Obligations & International Comity) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: SEAFDEC establishes the jurisdictional immunity of international intergovernmental organizations from domestic labor courts, ensuring that multilateral entities can perform their mandates free from local labor litigation.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 15

Callado vs. International Rice Research Institute (IRRI)

CITATION 244 SCRA 210, G.R. No. 106483, May 22, 1995 | Supreme Court Second Division (Romero, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Petitioner Ernesto Callado was employed as a driver by IRRI. While driving an IRRI vehicle on an official errand, he figured in a vehicular accident. Following an internal administrative investigation, IRRI terminated his employment. Callado filed a complaint for illegal dismissal before the NLRC. IRRI invoked its immunity under Presidential Decree No. 1620, which granted IRRI the status and immunities of an international organization. The Labor Arbiter dismissed the complaint, which the NLRC affirmed.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether IRRI enjoys immunity from suit and labor dispute proceedings under Presidential Decree No. 1620.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. IRRI is immune from suit and labor proceedings under PD 1620.

LEGAL BASIS Presidential Decree No. 1620, Section 3 provides: 'The Institute shall enjoy immunity from every form of legal process, except in so far as in any particular case it has expressly waived its immunity.' Article XVI, Section 3 and Article II, Section 2 of the Constitution.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court affirmed that the grant of diplomatic immunities and privileges to international organizations is a standard international practice to prevent harassment and maintain institutional independence. PD 1620 expressly confers absolute immunity from all legal processes upon IRRI. There was no showing of any waiver of immunity by IRRI. Employees of international organizations are bound by internal grievance and dispute resolution mechanisms provided in their institutional charters.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition and AFFIRMED the NLRC resolution upholding IRRI's immunity.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

§ Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Non-Suability of State & International Bodies) & Article II, Section 2 (General Principles of International Law) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Callado v. IRRI validates statutory and treaty grants of immunity to specialized international research institutes, reaffirming that host-state labor statutes yield to valid diplomatic immunity legislation.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 16

Department of Foreign Affairs vs. NLRC

CITATION 262 SCRA 39, G.R. No. 113191, September 18, 1996 | Supreme Court Second Division (Vitug, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Jose C. Magnayi was hired as a consultant by the Asian Development Bank (ADB). When his contract was pre-terminated, Magnayi filed a complaint for illegal dismissal before the Labor Arbiter. ADB did not submit to jurisdiction, and the DFA sent a formal diplomatic letter to the Labor Arbiter stating that ADB enjoys immunity from legal process under the ADB Headquarters Agreement. Despite this, the Labor Arbiter rendered a default judgment against ADB. The DFA filed a petition for certiorari before the Supreme Court.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the DFA's diplomatic certification of immunity of an international organization is conclusive and binding upon domestic labor tribunals.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The DFA's official recognition and certification of diplomatic and sovereign immunity is conclusive upon domestic courts and administrative bodies.

LEGAL BASIS Section 44 of the Agreement between the Republic of the Philippines and the ADB (ADB Headquarters Agreement); Article XVI, Section 3 and Article VII, Section 21 of the Constitution.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the determination of whether an entity is entitled to diplomatic or sovereign immunity is a political question exclusively vested in the Executive Department through the Department of Foreign Affairs. When the DFA formally conveys to a court or quasi-judicial body that an entity possesses immunity pursuant to a ratified international treaty, the tribunal is duty-bound to respect that determination and dismiss the proceeding. The Labor Arbiter committed grave abuse of discretion by ignoring the DFA's diplomatic communication.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, ANNULLED the Labor Arbiter's default decision, and DISMISSED the labor case against ADB.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Immunity from Suit) & Article VII, Section 20/21 (Executive Conduct of Foreign Relations) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: DFA v. NLRC establishes the 'Conclusiveness of Executive Certification Doctrine' in sovereign and diplomatic immunity cases. The judiciary must defer to the executive's foreign relations assessments.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 17

Fontanilla vs. Maliaman

CITATION 194 SCRA 486, G.R. No. 55963 & 61045, February 27, 1991 | Supreme Court En Banc (Paras, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

A pickup truck owned by the National Irrigation Administration (NIA) and driven by its regular employee, Hugo Garcia, bumped and killed Francisco Fontanilla, a young boy riding a bicycle. The boy's parents filed a damage suit against Garcia and the NIA. The trial court held NIA solidarily liable. NIA argued that as a government agency, it was immune from tort liability under Article 2180 of the Civil Code because it did not act through a 'special agent.'

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the National Irrigation Administration (NIA), a government-owned corporation, is immune from tort liability for damages caused by its employee.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. NIA is an incorporated government corporation performing proprietary functions and is liable for torts committed by its regular employees.

LEGAL BASIS Section 2(f) of Republic Act No. 3601 (NIA Charter) granting it the power 'to sue and be sued'; Article 2180 of the Civil Code; Article XVI, Section 3 of the Constitution.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that government agencies are categorized into unincorporated (performing governmental functions) and incorporated (with corporate personality). NIA is a government corporation created with a distinct legal personality endowed with the statutory capacity to sue and be sued. Its functions (irrigation service fees, infrastructure construction) are proprietary and commercial in nature. When the State creates a corporation to engage in business-like activities, it descends to the level of an ordinary enterprise and sheds its sovereign immunity, rendering it liable for torts and damages.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the decision holding NIA solidarily liable for damages for the wrongful death of the victim.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (State Immunity & Corporate Waiver) & Article 2180 Civil Code (Vicarious Tort Liability) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Fontanilla v. Maliaman establishes that statutory 'sue and be sued' charter clauses strip government corporations of sovereign tort immunity when engaged in proprietary operations.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 18

Farolan vs. Court of Tax Appeals

CITATION 217 SCRA 298, G.R. No. 42204, January 21, 1993 | Supreme Court Third Division (Romero, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Bagong Buhay Trading imported 63 bales of screen nets. The Bureau of Customs (BOC) seized the goods for alleged tariff violations. The CTA later ordered the release of the goods. When the importer sought delivery, only part of the shipment could be located, with the rest lost while in customs custody. The CTA ordered the Acting Commissioner of Customs (Farolan) and the Collector of Customs to pay the value of the missing goods.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the Bureau of Customs and its officers can be held liable for the value of missing goods lost while in official customs storage.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. The Bureau of Customs is an unincorporated government agency performing purely governmental functions and enjoys state immunity from damage liability.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Section 3 of the Constitution; Doctrine of Non-Suability of Unincorporated Government Agencies performing sovereign functions.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the Bureau of Customs is an unincorporated agency of the national government exercising sovereign functions of taxation and customs regulation. A suit for monetary damages against the Bureau of Customs is essentially a suit against the Republic of the Philippines without its consent. Public officers are also not personally liable for loss or damages in the performance of governmental duties unless there is proof of bad faith, malice, or gross negligence, which was not established.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court REVERSED the CTA decision and ABSOLVED the customs officials and the Bureau from monetary liability.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Immunity of Unincorporated Government Agencies) & Article VI, Section 28 (Taxation Power) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Farolan illustrates the fundamental distinction between incorporated state corporations (which can be held liable) and unincorporated line bureaus performing sovereign duties (which remain fully shielded by state immunity).

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 19

China National Machinery & Equipment Corp. vs. Santamaria

CITATION 665 SCRA 189, G.R. No. 185572, February 7, 2012 | Supreme Court First Division (Sereno, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Northrail entered into a contract with China National Machinery & Equipment Corp. (CNMEG), a Chinese state-owned enterprise, for the construction of the North Luzon Railways Project, financed by a loan from the Export-Import Bank of China. Respondents filed a complaint before the RTC seeking to annul the Northrail-CNMEG contract for lack of public bidding. CNMEG moved to dismiss, claiming sovereign state immunity as an agent and instrumentality of the People's Republic of China.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether CNMEG, a Chinese state-owned corporation, is entitled to claim sovereign state immunity from suit in Philippine courts.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. CNMEG is a state-owned commercial corporation engaged in a proprietary commercial contract (jure gestionis) and is not immune from suit.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Section 3 of the Constitution and the Restrictive Doctrine of Sovereign Immunity under Public International Law.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that under the restrictive theory of sovereign immunity, state immunity extends only to acts jure imperii. CNMEG is a distinct commercial entity with separate juridical personality, engaged in construction and commercial contracting for profit. Entering into a commercial railway construction agreement is an act jure gestionis. When a foreign state or its state corporation enters into commercial contracts with private parties or instrumentalities, it descends to the level of an ordinary contractor and waives its immunity.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DENIED CNMEG's petition and AFFIRMED the RTC orders denying the motion to dismiss.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

§ Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Restrictive Immunity Doctrine) & Article II, Section 2 (International Commercial Law Principles) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: China National Machinery reaffirms the Restrictive Theory of Sovereign Immunity in high-value public infrastructure projects, holding that foreign state-owned commercial contractors cannot evade judicial scrutiny for business contracts.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 20

Civil Aeronautics Administration vs. Court of Appeals

CITATION 167 SCRA 28, G.R. No. L-51806, November 8, 1988 | Supreme Court Second Division (Paras, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Ernest E. Simke, an executive passenger, slipped and fell on an elevation drop on the viewing deck of the Manila International Airport (MIA), suffering serious bodily injuries. Simke filed an action for damages against the Civil Aeronautics Administration (CAA), which had operational control and maintenance of MIA. CAA claimed state immunity, arguing it was an unincorporated agency of the Department of Public Works and Communications.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the Civil Aeronautics Administration (CAA) can claim sovereign immunity from suit for damages resulting from improper airport maintenance.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. The operation and maintenance of an international airport is a proprietary function, rendering the CAA suable and liable for damages.

LEGAL BASIS Republic Act No. 776 (Civil Aeronautics Act); Article 2180 Civil Code; Article XVI, Section 3 of the Constitution.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that although the CAA was created as an unincorporated agency, its statutory charter authorized it to operate airports, lease commercial spaces, and charge terminal, landing, and viewing fees for profit. These operations are proprietary and commercial in nature. When the government engages in commercial enterprises (such as operating airports), it is treated as any other private enterprise and cannot invoke sovereign immunity to escape liability for negligence.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the judgment holding CAA liable for damages to the injured passenger.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Proprietary Functions Exception to Immunity) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: CAA v. CA highlights the functional test: even an unincorporated agency is suable if the specific activity giving rise to the cause of action is proprietary, commercial, and income-generating.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 21

Meritt vs. Government of the Philippine Islands

CITATION 34 Phil. 311, G.R. No. 11154, March 21, 1916 | Supreme Court (Trent, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

E. Meritt was severely injured when his motorcycle collided with an ambulance owned by the Philippine General Hospital driven by a government chauffeur. To enable Meritt to seek redress, the Philippine Legislature enacted Act No. 2457 authorizing Meritt to bring a suit against the Government of the Philippine Islands to fix the damages, if any. The trial court awarded damages to Meritt, but the Government appealed on the ground of lack of statutory liability.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the legislative enactment authorizing a person to sue the government (suability) constitutes an admission of substantive tort liability (liability).

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Statutory consent to be sued merely waives state immunity from suit (suability), but does not concede substantive liability.

LEGAL BASIS Act No. 2457; Article 1903 (now Article 2180) of the Civil Code; Classic Doctrine of Suability vs. Liability.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court articulated the bedrock distinction in state immunity law: Waiver of immunity from suit (suability) is merely a procedural concession opening the courthouse doors. It does not create a new cause of action or concede substantive liability. Liability is governed by substantive civil law. Under the Civil Code, the State is only liable for torts when acting through a 'special agent' specially commissioned to perform the act, and not for the acts of regular employees or chauffeurs.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court REVERSED the monetary award against the Government on the ground of lack of substantive liability under the special agent rule.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Dual Concept of Suability vs. Liability) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Meritt v. Government of the Philippine Islands is the classic landmark foundational precedent establishing that consent to be sued (suability) is entirely separate and distinct from the admission of liability.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 22

Republic vs. Feliciano

CITATION 148 SCRA 424, G.R. No. L-70853, March 12, 1987 | Supreme Court En Banc (Yap, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Private respondent Pablo Feliciano filed a complaint against the Republic of the Philippines represented by the Land Authority for recovery of ownership and possession of a parcel of land in Camarines Sur. The Republic moved to dismiss on the ground of non-suability of the State without its consent. The trial court denied the motion, ruling that state immunity cannot be used to perpetrate an injustice.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether a private citizen can maintain a suit for land recovery against the Republic of the Philippines without express legislative consent.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. The Republic cannot be sued without express statutory consent enacted by Congress.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Section 3 of the 1987 Constitution ('The State may not be sued without its consent'); Act No. 3083 and Commonwealth Act No. 327.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court ruled that the doctrine of non-suability of the State is a fundamental attribute of sovereignty. Consent to be sued must be express and unequivocal, which can only be granted through a statute enacted by Congress (or general laws like Act 3083 for money claims arising from contract). Vague equitable considerations cannot override the constitutional prohibition. A suit against a government agency to recover property or public funds is a suit against the State itself.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, ANNULLED the trial court orders, and ORDERED the DISMISSAL of the complaint.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Non-Suability of the State & Express Statutory Consent) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Republic v. Feliciano reaffirms the strict requirement of express legislative authorization for suits against the Republic, preserving state assets from unauthorized judicial interference.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 23

University of the Philippines vs. Dizon

CITATION 679 SCRA 54, G.R. No. 171182, August 23, 2012 | Supreme Court First Division (Bersamin, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

UP entered into a construction contract with Stern Builders for the UP Los Baños library building. Stern Builders sued UP for unpaid billings and damages. The RTC rendered judgment against UP and issued a writ of execution and notice of garnishment against UP's depository accounts with Land Bank and DBP. UP challenged the garnishment, contending that public funds of the national university cannot be garnished and that monetary claims must be filed with the Commission on Audit (COA).

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether the funds of the University of the Philippines are public funds exempt from garnishment and execution. 2. Whether money claims resulting from court judgments against state institutions must first be filed with the Commission on Audit (COA).

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER 1. YES. UP's funds are government funds exempt from garnishment. 2. YES. All money claims against the government must be filed with the COA under Presidential Decree No. 1445.

LEGAL BASIS Presidential Decree No. 1445 (Government Auditing Code of the Philippines); Republic Act No. 9500 (UP Charter); Article IX-D, Section 2(1) and Article XVI, Section 3 of the Constitution.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court ruled that UP is the national university and an instrumentality of the government. Its funds, including tuition fees and appropriations, are public funds. Even if UP can sue and be sued under its charter, judicial power ends with the rendition of the final judgment. Trial courts have no authority to issue writs of execution or garnishment against public bank accounts. The claimant must file a petition with the Commission on Audit pursuant to PD 1445, which has primary jurisdiction over the settlement of money claims against the State.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, QUASHED the writ of execution and notices of garnishment, and directed Stern Builders to file its money claim with the COA.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Exemption of Public Funds from Execution) & Article IX-D, Section 2(1) (Exclusive COA Money Claims Jurisdiction) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: UP v. Dizon establishes the vital two-stage rule for state litigation: obtaining a judgment against a suable state agency does not authorize execution; actual payment must undergo COA audit and legislative appropriation.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 24

City of Manila vs. Teotico

CITATION 22 SCRA 267, G.R. No. L-23052, January 29, 1968 | Supreme Court (Concepcion, C.J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Genaro Teotico was walking along P. Burgos Avenue in Manila to board a jeepney when he fell into an uncovered, unlighted catchbasin (manhole) on the street, sustaining fractured ribs and injuries. Teotico sued the City of Manila for damages. The City defended on the ground that under Section 4 of its City Charter (RA 409), the City is exempt from liability for damages caused by the failure of city officials to enforce laws or maintain public works.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the City of Manila is liable for damages under Article 2189 of the Civil Code, or exempt under Section 4 of its City Charter (RA 409).

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER The City of Manila is LIABLE under Article 2189 of the Civil Code, which prevails over the charter exemption.

LEGAL BASIS Article 2189 of the Civil Code explicitly provides: 'Provinces, cities and municipalities shall be liable for damages for the death of, or injuries suffered by, any person by reason of the defective condition of roads, streets, bridges, public buildings, and other public works under their control or supervision.'

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that Article 2189 of the Civil Code is a special substantive rule specifically governing municipal liability for defective public works, streets, and roads. While Section 4 of the Manila Charter contains a general exemption for failure to enforce laws, Article 2189 specifically creates statutory liability for defective public physical infrastructure. The City maintained control and supervision over P. Burgos Avenue and was negligent in leaving the manhole open and unguarded.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the decision holding the City of Manila liable for damages.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Statutory Waiver of Municipal Immunity) & Article X (Local Government Accountability) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: City of Manila v. Teotico establishes that local governments are statutorily liable under Article 2189 of the Civil Code for torts arising from defective roads and public infrastructure under their physical control.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 25

United States of America vs. Ruiz

CITATION 136 SCRA 487, G.R. No. L-35645, May 22, 1985 | Supreme Court En Banc (Abad Santos, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The United States invited bids for the repair and construction of wharves at Subic Bay Naval Station. Eligible contractors submitted bids, including private respondent Eligio de Guzman & Co. The US naval authorities rejected De Guzman's bid. De Guzman filed a complaint in the CFI of Rizal for specific performance and damages against the USA. The US moved to dismiss invoking sovereign state immunity, which the trial court denied.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the United States of America waived its sovereign immunity by entering into a contract for the repair of wharves at a military naval base.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. The contract for naval base wharf repair is an act jure imperii (sovereign military defense function), and the US is immune from suit.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Section 3 of the Constitution and the Restrictive Doctrine of Sovereign Immunity in Public International Law.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the restrictive doctrine distinguishes between commercial contracts (jure gestionis) and sovereign contracts (jure imperii). A contract for the repair of naval base wharves is inextricably linked to military defense and national security operations of the US armed forces. It is not a commercial or retail trading contract for profit. Therefore, the US acted in its sovereign capacity (jure imperii) and remained fully immune from domestic court jurisdiction.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, ANNULLED the trial court orders, and DISMISSED the civil complaint against the USA.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Sovereign Acts Jure Imperii) & Article II, Section 2 (International Law Doctrines) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: USA v. Ruiz is the bedrock Philippine decision elucidating that contracts directly supporting military installations and national defense are sovereign (jure imperii) rather than commercial, preserving absolute state immunity.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 26

Froilan vs. Pan Oriental Shipping Company

CITATION 95 Phil. 905, G.R. No. L-6060, September 30, 1954 | Supreme Court (Paras, C.J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Fernando Froilan purchased a vessel (FS-197) from the Shipping Administration, paying partly in cash with the balance secured by a chattel mortgage. Froilan defaulted, and the government repossessed the vessel and chartered it to Pan Oriental Shipping. Froilan sued the Government to recover the vessel. The Government, through the Republic, filed a complaint in intervention seeking repossession and foreclosure. Pan Oriental filed an answer in intervention with a counterclaim against the Republic for repair expenses incurred on the vessel. The Republic moved to dismiss the counterclaim on the ground of state immunity.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the Republic of the Philippines impliedly waives its state immunity from suit when it voluntarily files a complaint or complaint-in-intervention in a civil action.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. By voluntarily initiating an affirmative action in court, the Government sheds its immunity and opens itself to compulsory counterclaims.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Section 3 of the Constitution; Doctrine of Implied Waiver of State Immunity.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court ruled that when the State voluntarily initiates legal proceedings or intervenes to assert affirmative claims against a private party, it descends to the level of an ordinary litigant. The State cannot claim sovereign immunity as a shield against compulsory counterclaims arising from the same transaction. Having submitted to the court's jurisdiction to obtain affirmative relief, the State is bound by the rules of fair play and must answer counterclaims directly connected to the main action.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court HELD that the trial court properly entertained the counterclaim against the Republic arising from the vessel repairs.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Implied Waiver of State Immunity by Voluntary Initiation of Action) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Froilan v. Pan Oriental Shipping is the classic authority on implied waiver of immunity: when the State files an affirmative suit, it waives its immunity against germane counterclaims.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 27

Municipality of San Miguel vs. Fernandez

CITATION 130 SCRA 56, G.R. No. L-61744, June 25, 1984 | Supreme Court Second Division (Relova, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The CFI of Bulacan rendered a money judgment against the Municipality of San Miguel in an expropriation/damage case. To enforce the judgment, the trial judge issued an order of execution against municipal funds deposited in the municipal treasury and provincial bank accounts. The Municipality challenged the order, arguing that municipal public funds are exempt from execution.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether municipal and public government funds are subject to execution and garnishment to satisfy a court judgment.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Municipal and public funds are held in trust for the public and are strictly exempt from execution and garnishment.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Section 3 and Article VI, Section 29(1) of the Constitution ('No money shall be paid out of the Treasury except in pursuance of an appropriation made by law').

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that all government revenues and municipal funds are collected for public purposes and held in trust for public services (health, peace and order, sanitation). Even though a municipality can be sued under its corporate charter, consent to be sued is not consent to execution. Permitting private litigants to garnish municipal treasury accounts would paralyze essential government operations and violate the constitutional mandate that public money requires a legislative appropriation.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, ANNULLED the writ of execution, and PERMANENTLY RESTRAINED the execution against municipal funds.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Non-Execution of Public Funds) & Article VI, Section 29(1) (Appropriation Requirement) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: San Miguel v. Fernandez firmly sets the rule that public funds are immune from levy and garnishment, preventing private judgments from bankrupting local government functions.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 28

Municipality of Makati vs. Court of Appeals

CITATION 190 SCRA 206, G.R. Nos. 89898-99, October 1, 1990 | Supreme Court Third Division (Cortés, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Municipality of Makati expropriated properties belonging to Admiral Finance Creditors Consortium for a public road widening project. The RTC fixed just compensation at P5.29 million. When Makati failed to pay the balance, the trial court ordered the garnishment of Makati's depository bank accounts with the Philippine National Bank (PNB). Makati assailed the garnishment before the CA and the Supreme Court.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the bank accounts of the Municipality of Makati may be garnished without a prior appropriation ordinance enacted by the Sangguniang Bayan.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Public funds deposited in municipal bank accounts cannot be garnished without a prior legislative appropriation ordinance.

LEGAL BASIS Article VI, Section 29(1) of the Constitution; Presidential Decree No. 477 (Local Fiscal Administration); Article XVI, Section 3 of the Constitution.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that public funds in bank accounts belonging to local government units cannot be garnished. Payment of money judgments against an LGU requires the enactment of an appropriation ordinance by the local legislative council (Sanggunian). However, the Court also ruled that the LGU cannot indefinitely refuse to pay just compensation for expropriated land. The municipality was ordered to immediately pass an appropriation ordinance to satisfy the final expropriation judgment.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court LIFTED the garnishment order, but ORDERED the Municipality of Makati to immediately enact the necessary appropriation ordinance to pay the full just compensation.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (Immunity of Public Funds), Article III, Section 9 (Just Compensation), & Article VI, Section 29(1) (Appropriation Ordinance) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Makati v. CA balances the rule of non-garnishment of public funds with the constitutional guarantee of just compensation, providing that mandamus lies to compel an LGU to enact an appropriation ordinance to pay expropriation judgments.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 29

Wylie vs. Rarang

CITATION 209 SCRA 357, G.R. No. 74135, May 28, 1992 | Supreme Court Third Division (Gutierrez, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

M.H. Wylie and James Williams were US naval officers stationed at the US Naval Base in Subic Bay. In an official base newsletter, they published a defamatory article accusing private respondent Aurora Rarang, a Filipino civilian employee, of being a 'mercantile thief.' Rarang filed an action for damages for defamation before the RTC of Olongapo. Petitioners claimed official state immunity under the Military Bases Agreement.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether US military officers are shielded by state immunity from civil damage liability for publishing defamatory statements against a Filipino civilian employee.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):
