ANSWER NO. State immunity cannot be used as a shield to perpetrate personal torts and injustices; public officers committing ultra vires defamatory acts are personally liable.

LEGAL BASIS Article XVI, Section 3 of the Constitution; Article 20 and 2176 of the Civil Code; Doctrine of Personal Liability of Public Officers for Ultra Vires Torts.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the doctrine of state immunity was never intended to serve as a blanket license for foreign or domestic officials to commit torts and defamatory acts with impunity. Publishing a false and malicious libel naming a civilian employee as a thief is ultra vires and completely outside the scope of official military authority. When a public officer exceeds lawful authority and commits a personal tort, the officer is stripped of official protection and becomes personally liable in damages.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the personal damage liability of petitioners Wylie and Williams, while absolving the United States Government.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XVI, Section 3 (State Immunity Limits & Exception to Injustice) & Article II, Section 1 (Rule of Law & Human Rights) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Wylie v. Rarang establishes the paramount principle that state immunity is not an absolute cloak for tortious misconduct. When an official acts maliciously or ultra vires, the cloak falls away and personal civil liability attaches.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

TOPIC III: THE FUNDAMENTAL POWERS OF THE STATE

Police Power, Eminent Domain (Just Compensation), and Taxation

★ TOPIC III • KEY CONSTITUTIONAL DOCTRINES & LECTURE OUTLINE • Police Power Primacy: Inherently legislative power to promote the general welfare; administrative coordinating bodies lack police power without statutory grant (MMDA v. Bel-Air). • Police Power vs. Eminent Domain: Mandatory welfare price discounts (e.g. 20% senior/PWD discount) are police power regulations, not compensable takings (Southern Luzon Drug Corp.). • Eminent Domain Requisites: Genuine public use, prompt just compensation, and due process (Salem Investment, Ardona). • Right of Reversion & 5-Year Rule: Abandonment of public use entitles owners to repurchase (MCIAA v. Lozada, Salud Jimenez); non-payment within 5 years entitles owners to recover possession (Republic v. Lim). • Judicial Determination of Just Compensation: Exclusive judicial power pegged to fair market value at taking plus legal interest (DPWH v. Tecson, LBP v. Dalauta, Alfonso v. LBP).
CASE 30

MMDA vs. Bel-Air Village Association, Inc.

CITATION 328 SCRA 836, G.R. No. 135962, March 27, 2000 | Supreme Court First Division (Puno, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Metropolitan Manila Development Authority (MMDA) issued a notice to Bel-Air Village Association, Inc. (BAVA) demanding the opening of Neptune Street in Bel-Air Village to vehicular traffic to ease congestion along EDSA and Buendia Avenue, threatening to demolish the perimeter wall. BAVA filed an injunction suit before the RTC of Makati, claiming that MMDA lacked police power or statutory authority to open private subdivision roads. The RTC and CA issued an injunction against MMDA.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the MMDA has the delegated police power or legislative authority to open private roads and demolish subdivision perimeter walls.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. MMDA is an administrative development and coordinating body; it does not possess police power or legislative ordinance-making authority.

LEGAL BASIS Republic Act No. 7924 (MMDA Charter); Article VI, Section 1 (Police Power is inherently legislative); Article X, Section 5 (Local Autonomy).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court ruled that police power - the inherent power of the State to regulate liberty and property for the general welfare - is inherently legislative. It can be delegated only to representative legislative bodies such as Congress and local legislative councils (Sanggunian). RA 7924 created the MMDA as an administrative coordinating body for delivery of metro-wide basic services. RA 7924 contains no grant of police power or legislative power to enact ordinances. Therefore, MMDA cannot unilaterally order the opening of private subdivision roads or demolish private walls without an ordinance enacted by the Sangguniang Panlungsod.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the decision of the Court of Appeals enjoining MMDA from opening Neptune Street.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VI, Section 1 (Inherent Legislative Nature of Police Power) & Article X, Section 5 (LGU Legislative Delegations) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: MMDA v. Bel-Air is the leading case on the nature and delegation of Police Power. It establishes that administrative agencies cannot exercise police power in the absence of an explicit legislative grant, maintaining the strict boundary between administrative coordination and legislative regulation.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 31

Philippine Association of Service Exporters, Inc. (PASEI) vs. Drilon

CITATION 163 SCRA 386, G.R. No. 81958, June 30, 1988 | Supreme Court En Banc (Sarmiento, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Department of Labor and Employment (DOLE) Secretary Franklin Drilon issued Department Order No. 1, Series of 1988, temporarily suspending the deployment of female domestic workers (domestic helpers) abroad following rampant reports of physical, sexual, and emotional abuse suffered by Filipina domestic workers in foreign countries. PASEI challenged the order as unconstitutional, alleging violation of the right to travel, due process, and equal protection of the laws.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether Department Order No. 1 is a valid exercise of police power for the protection of overseas female workers.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. DOLE Department Order No. 1 is a valid, reasonable, and constitutional exercise of police power.

LEGAL BASIS Article II, Section 14 (State Protection of Women); Article XIII, Section 3 (Labor Protection Clause); Article III, Section 1 (Due Process & Equal Protection).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the concept of police power allows the State to enact protective regulations to safeguard public welfare and vulnerable classes. The classification between male and female workers, and between domestic helpers and other professionals, rested on substantial distinctions: female domestic helpers working in private foreign households are particularly isolated and vulnerable to severe abuse and exploitation. The temporary suspension was a necessary and reasonable emergency measure to protect their fundamental human rights, which outweighs the private economic interests of recruitment agencies.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition and SUSTAINED the constitutionality of DOLE Department Order No. 1.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article II, Section 14 (Role of Women & Protection), Article XIII, Section 3 (Full Protection to Labor), & Article III, Section 1 (Equal Protection) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: PASEI v. Drilon demonstrates the expansive reach of Police Power in labor and social justice, establishing that the State may regulate employment and travel to protect the welfare of vulnerable citizens based on valid classifications.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 32

Tio vs. Videogram Regulatory Board

CITATION 151 SCRA 208, G.R. No. L-75697, June 18, 1987 | Supreme Court En Banc (Melencio-Herrera, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Petitioner Valentin Tio challenged the constitutionality of Presidential Decree No. 1987, which created the Videogram Regulatory Board (VRB) and imposed a 30% tax on the gross receipts of videogram operators. Tio argued that the 30% tax was excessive, confiscatory, and an unconstitutional rider beyond the subject expressed in the title of the decree.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether PD 1987 is a valid exercise of both the police power and the taxing power of the State to regulate the videogram industry and curb video piracy.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Taxation may be validly utilized as an instrument of police power to regulate an industry, curb piracy, and promote public morals.

LEGAL BASIS Article VI, Section 28 (Taxing Power for Regulation); Article VI, Section 26(1) (One Subject-One Title Rule); Police Power of the State.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that taxation is not solely for raising revenue; it may be legitimately used as an implement of police power for regulation. The proliferation of unregulated videograms had devastated the local movie industry and fostered rampant piracy and pornography. The 30% tax was designed to equalize economic burdens between the cinema and video industries and deter illegal reproduction. The title of PD 1987 was comprehensive enough to encompass regulatory taxation as a germane means to achieve its regulatory objective.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition and UPHELD the constitutionality of PD 1987.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VI, Section 28 (Regulatory Taxation), Article VI, Section 26(1) (Subject & Title of Bills), & Police Power Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Tio v. VRB illustrates the synergistic convergence of Police Power and Taxation (Regulatory Tax / Sumptuary Tax), where taxing power is used to achieve regulatory, moral, and economic policy goals.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 33

Acebedo Optical Company, Inc. vs. Court of Appeals

CITATION 329 SCRA 314, G.R. No. 100152, March 31, 2000 | Supreme Court En Banc (Purisima, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Acebedo Optical applied for a business permit to operate an optical shop in Iligan City. City Mayor Camilo Cabili issued the permit subject to a special condition that Acebedo shall not examine eyes or prescribe eyeglasses and shall only sell optical frames and lenses. When Acebedo hired licensed optometrists to examine patients inside its store, the Mayor revoked its business permit. Acebedo challenged the revocation before the courts.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether a city mayor, in the exercise of delegated police power to issue business permits, can impose professional practice conditions beyond what is regulated by national law (Optometry Law).

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. A city mayor's delegated police power is administrative and cannot override or impose restrictions beyond national professional statutes.

LEGAL BASIS Republic Act No. 1998 (The Optometry Law of 1957) and Republic Act No. 8050 (Revised Optometry Law); Section 455(b)(3)(iv) of the Local Government Code; Article X, Section 5.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the power to issue business permits is a delegated police power exercised for administrative regulation. A city mayor has no statutory power to regulate the practice of optometry or prohibit a corporate entity from hiring licensed optometrists to practice their profession, which is exclusively regulated by the Professional Regulation Commission (PRC) under national law. The special condition imposed on Acebedo's permit was ultra vires, arbitrary, and void. Consequently, the revocation based on the violation of an invalid condition was unlawful.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court REVERSED the CA decision and ORDERED the reinstatement of Acebedo's business permit.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article X, Section 5 (Scope of LGU Delegated Police Power) & Article III, Section 1 (Substantive Due Process against Ultra Vires Acts) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Acebedo Optical defines the strict boundaries of local delegated police power: local executives cannot enact conditions or regulations that conflict with national professional regulatory legislation.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 34

Taxicab Operators of Metro Manila vs. Board of Transportation

CITATION 117 SCRA 597, G.R. No. L-59234, September 30, 1982 | Supreme Court En Banc (Melencio-Herrera, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Board of Transportation (BOT) issued Memorandum Circular No. 77-42 phasing out taxicabs that were more than six (6) years old in Metro Manila. Taxicab operators assailed the circular, alleging that it violated equal protection because it applied exclusively to Metro Manila and not to other cities, and violated substantive due process by depriving them of their property without compensation.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the phase-out of taxicabs older than six years in Metro Manila is a valid exercise of police power and complies with equal protection.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The phase-out regulation is a valid exercise of police power for commuter safety, traffic efficiency, and environmental protection.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 1 (Due Process and Equal Protection of the Laws); Public Service Act (Commonwealth Act No. 146); Police Power of the State.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court ruled that the operation of public utilities is vested with public interest and subject to pervasive state regulation under police power. The six-year age limit was reasonable to protect commuter safety and curb air pollution caused by deteriorated vehicles. The geographical classification limiting the rule to Metro Manila was valid because Metro Manila experienced uniquely heavy traffic density, severe air pollution, and continuous vehicle wear-and-tear compared to provincial areas. The classification was based on substantial distinctions germane to the purpose.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition and SUSTAINED the validity of BOT Memorandum Circular No. 77-42.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 1 (Equal Protection & Police Power Classification) & Article XII, Section 11 (Public Utility Regulation) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Taxicab Operators v. BOT validates geographical and operational classifications in police power regulations over public utilities when justified by distinct regional safety and environmental conditions.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 35

Del Mar vs. Philippine Amusement and Gaming Corporation (PAGCOR)

CITATION 346 SCRA 485, G.R. No. 138298, November 29, 2000 | Supreme Court En Banc (Puno, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

PAGCOR entered into an agreement with Belle Jai Alai Corporation (Belle) and Filipinas Gaming Entertainment Totalizator Corp. (Filgame) to operate and manage jai-alai games and off-fronton betting stations. Raoul B. Del Mar and others filed petitions for prohibition, arguing that PAGCOR's statutory charter (PD 1869) does not authorize it to operate jai-alai or franchise private corporations to operate gambling games.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether PAGCOR has the statutory authority under its charter (PD 1869) to operate jai-alai and franchise private corporate joint ventures to conduct gambling.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Gambling is prohibited by general penal law, and any legislative franchise or exemption must be strictly construed and expressly granted.

LEGAL BASIS Presidential Decree No. 1869 (PAGCOR Charter); Article VI, Section 1 (Legislative Power to Franchise Gambling); Police Power over Public Morals.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that gambling is fundamentally injurious to public morals and general welfare, and is prohibited under the Revised Penal Code. A legislative franchise authorizing gambling is a derogation of general police power policy and must be strictly construed against the grantee. PD 1869 granted PAGCOR the authority to operate casinos and gambling games generally played in casino clubs, but did not include jai-alai (which had a separate, expired franchise under PD 810). Moreover, PAGCOR cannot delegate or sub-license its sovereign franchise to private joint ventures.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition and ENJOINED PAGCOR and private respondents from operating jai-alai games and off-fronton betting.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VI, Section 1 (Legislative Franchising Authority) & Police Power over Public Morals & Prohibited Games Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Del Mar v. PAGCOR establishes the doctrine of strict construction of gambling franchises under Police Power: because gambling is contrary to public policy, statutory exemptions must be express, unequivocal, and non-delegable.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 36

Angeles University Foundation vs. City of Angeles

CITATION 675 SCRA 359, G.R. No. 189999, June 27, 2012 | Supreme Court First Division (Villarama, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Angeles University Foundation (AUF), an educational non-stock, non-profit institution, undertook construction of an 11-story medical center. The City of Angeles assessed AUF building permit fees and related regulatory charges amounting to P326,000. AUF claimed tax exemption under Section 8 of Republic Act No. 6055 (Non-stock Educational Institutions Law) and Article XIV, Section 4(3) of the 1987 Constitution.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether building permit fees imposed under the National Building Code are taxes from which non-profit educational institutions are exempt, or regulatory police power fees.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER Building permit fees are REGULATORY FEES levied under the Police Power, NOT taxes; therefore, educational institutions are NOT exempt.

LEGAL BASIS Article XIV, Section 4(3) of the Constitution; Presidential Decree No. 1096 (National Building Code of the Philippines); Fundamental Distinction between Taxing Power and Police Power.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court articulated the clear legal distinction between a tax (levied primarily for revenue generation under the Taxing Power) and a regulatory fee (levied primarily for regulation, inspection, and public safety under the Police Power). Building permit fees under the National Building Code are collected to defray the administrative cost of inspecting structural safety, sanitation, and fire compliance. They are police power impositions. Constitutional and statutory tax exemptions granted to educational institutions apply strictly to taxes, not to police power regulatory fees.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DENIED AUF's petition and AFFIRMED its liability to pay the building permit fees.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XIV, Section 4(3) (Tax Exemption of Educational Institutions) & Police Power vs. Taxing Power Distinction Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Angeles University Foundation is the definitive authority distinguishing Taxing Power from Police Power: constitutional tax exemptions protect only against revenue taxes and never grant immunity from regulatory police power fees.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 37

Southern Luzon Drug Corporation vs. DSWD

CITATION 809 SCRA 315, G.R. No. 199669, April 25, 2017 | Supreme Court En Banc (Reyes, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Republic Act No. 9257 (Expanded Senior Citizens Act of 2003) and RA 9442 (Magna Carta for Disabled Persons) granted a mandatory 20% sales discount to senior citizens and PWDs on medicine purchases, treating the discount as a 'tax deduction' from gross income rather than a 'tax credit' from tax due. Southern Luzon Drug Corporation challenged the law, asserting that compelling private drugstores to shoulder the 20% discount without a dollar-for-dollar tax credit amounted to an unconstitutional taking of private property without just compensation under Eminent Domain.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the mandatory 20% Senior Citizen and PWD discount is an exercise of Eminent Domain requiring just compensation or a valid exercise of Police Power.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER It is a legitimate exercise of POLICE POWER, and treating the discount as a tax deduction is constitutionally sufficient.

LEGAL BASIS Article II, Section 9 (Promotion of General Welfare); Article XIII, Section 11 (Health & Welfare of the Elderly and Disabled); Article III, Section 1 & Section 9.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the 20% discount is a price regulation measure enacted pursuant to the State's police power to promote the social justice and health of seniors and PWDs. Police power regulates the use and pricing of property for public welfare without requiring compensation, whereas eminent domain involves the actual taking of private property for public use. The State did not take ownership of the medicines. The tax deduction mechanism provided by Congress is a matter of legislative grace and adequately mitigates the business impact. The regulation was reasonable, proportional, and fully constitutional.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition and UPHELD the constitutionality of the 20% discount as a tax deduction under RA 9257 and RA 9442.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XIII, Section 11 (Social Justice for Elderly & PWDs), Article III, Section 1 (Police Power), & Article III, Section 9 (Eminent Domain Distinction) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Southern Luzon Drug Corp. establishes the doctrinal benchmark differentiating Police Power from Eminent Domain in social legislation: mandatory statutory welfare price discounts are police power regulations, not compensable takings of property.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 38

Republic vs. Salem Investment Corporation

CITATION 334 SCRA 320, G.R. No. 137569, June 23, 2000 | Supreme Court Second Division (Mendoza, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Republic sought to expropriate several parcels of land along EDSA in Pasay City for the construction of the Light Rail Transit (LRT) Line 1 extension. Salem Investment Corporation owned one of the lots. During the expropriation proceedings, the government deposited provisional amounts and took possession. Issues arose as to the proper determination of ownership, title validity, and the precise valuation of just compensation.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

What are the mandatory requisites for the valid exercise of the power of eminent domain and judicial determination of just compensation.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER Eminent domain requires: (1) genuine public use/purpose, (2) payment of just compensation, and (3) observance of due process.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 of the 1987 Constitution ('Private property shall not be taken for public use without just compensation'); Rule 67 of the Rules of Court.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court ruled that the power of eminent domain is the inherent sovereign right of the State to expropriate private property for public use upon payment of just compensation. Public infrastructure projects such as mass rapid transit systems (LRT Line 1) represent quintessentially valid public uses. However, the State cannot retain title or permanent possession without paying the full fair market value determined by the trial court with the assistance of commissioners. Conflicting ownership claims must be resolved by the expropriation court before disbursement of the compensation proceeds.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court REMANDED the case to the trial court for the definitive determination of ownership and final calculation of just compensation.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Eminent Domain Requisites & Just Compensation) & Article III, Section 1 (Due Process in Expropriation) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Republic v. Salem Investment reiterates the essential constitutional tripod of Eminent Domain: valid public use, full and timely just compensation, and strict judicial due process.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 39

Estate of Salud Jimenez vs. Philippine Export Zone Authority (PEZA)

CITATION 349 SCRA 240, G.R. No. 137285, January 16, 2001 | Supreme Court Second Division (De Leon, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

PEZA expropriated a 12,228 square meter parcel of land belonging to the Estate of Salud Jimenez in Rosario, Cavite, for the development of an export processing zone. During the proceedings, PEZA entered into a compromise agreement to swap the lot with another property. Later, PEZA failed to deliver the swap property and sought to withdraw the expropriation as to that lot. The Estate demanded the return of the property or payment of current market value.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the expropriating authority can abandon or withdraw expropriation proceedings and what rights accrue to the landowner upon abandonment of the public purpose.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. When the public purpose for which property was taken is abandoned or ceases, the landowner has the right to reacquire the property.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 of the Constitution; Doctrine of Abandonment of Public Purpose in Expropriation.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the taking of private property under eminent domain is strictly conditioned upon its actual devoted use for a specified public purpose. Expropriation is not an absolute, unconditional transfer of fee simple title when the expropriation is undertaken for a specific statutory public project. If the expropriating agency abandons the public purpose or determines that the land is no longer needed, title does not remain permanently with the State; the previous landowner is entitled to recover the property upon returning the compensation received.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court ORDERED PEZA to reconvey the lot to the Estate of Salud Jimenez upon the return of the provisional deposit.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Eminent Domain: Condition of Continuing Public Use & Right of Reversion) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Salud Jimenez establishes the vital Doctrine of Reversion: eminent domain is perpetually tied to public use; when the public purpose is abandoned, the original owner is entitled to repurchase or recover the land.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 40

Mactan-Cebu International Airport Authority (MCIAA) vs. Lozada, Jr.

CITATION 613 SCRA 618, G.R. No. 176625, February 25, 2010 | Supreme Court Second Division (Nachura, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Civil Aeronautics Administration (predecessor of MCIAA) expropriated Lot No. 88 belonging to Bernardo Lozada, Jr. in Lahug, Cebu City, for the expansion of Lahug Airport, with an express verbal assurance that if the airport expansion was abandoned or moved to Mactan, the owners could repurchase the lot at the expropriation price. The Lahug Airport was subsequently decommissioned and converted into a commercial IT park (Cebu IT Park). Lozada demanded to repurchase the property. MCIAA refused, claiming its title was absolute.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether former landowners have the right to repurchase expropriated land when the public purpose (airport expansion) is completely abandoned.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Landowners have the right of repurchase upon abandonment of the public purpose for which the property was expropriated.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 of the 1987 Constitution; Doctrine of Resolutory Condition and Constructive Trust in Eminent Domain.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that taking under eminent domain is subject to the resolutory condition that the property will be used for the specified public purpose. The State holds expropriated land in trust for that public use. When MCIAA permanently abandoned the Lahug airport operations and converted the area into a commercial IT park, the public purpose ceased to exist. To permit the State to keep the land or sell it for private commercial profit would violate substantive due process and the fundamental foundation of eminent domain. Lozada was entitled to repurchase the property at the original expropriation price, subject to reimbursement of necessary expenses.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court ORDERED MCIAA to reconvey Lot No. 88 to the Lozadas upon payment of the expropriation price received.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Eminent Domain & Resolutory Condition of Public Purpose) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: MCIAA v. Lozada firmly reinforces the doctrine that eminent domain title is held in constructive trust; abandonment of the public project activates the landowner's constitutional right of repurchase.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 41

Republic vs. Lim

CITATION 462 SCRA 265, G.R. No. 161656, June 29, 2005 | Supreme Court Second Division (Sandoval-Gutierrez, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

In 1938, the Government expropriated Lots 932 and 939 in Lahug, Cebu City, for military use (Camp Lapu-Lapu). The title was never transferred to the Republic, and the government failed to pay the just compensation fixed by the court for over fifty (50) years. In 2001, respondent Vicente Lim, successor-in-interest of the original owner, filed an action to quiet title and recover possession. The Republic claimed that title passed upon expropriation judgment and non-payment only gave rise to an action to collect money.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether non-payment of just compensation for an unreasonable length of time (5 years or more) entitles the owner to recover possession of the expropriated property.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Non-payment of just compensation within 5 years from finality of the expropriation judgment entitles the owner to recover possession.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 of the Constitution; Requisite of Prompt Payment in Just Compensation; 5-Year Prescriptive Rule.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court laid down the landmark '5-Year Rule' in Eminent Domain. The Court held that title to expropriated property does not pass to the State until just compensation is fully paid. While the general rule is that non-payment only warrants an action for damages or collection, an exception applies where the government fails to pay just compensation within five (5) years from the finality of the expropriation judgment. Here, 57 years had elapsed without payment. Such prolonged failure constitutes an outrageous confiscation of private property without due process, entitling the owner to the recovery of possession.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court ORDERED the Republic to pay full just compensation with interest within 6 months, failing which possession shall be restored to respondent Lim.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Prompt Just Compensation Requirement) & Article III, Section 1 (Due Process against Confiscation) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Republic v. Lim established the famous '5-Year Rule': non-payment of just compensation within five years from finality of judgment invalidates the taking and empowers the owner to recover possession.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 42

Heirs of Alberto Suguitan vs. City of Mandaluyong

CITATION 328 SCRA 137, G.R. No. 135087, March 14, 2000 | Supreme Court Third Division (Gonzaga-Reyes, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Sangguniang Panlungsod of Mandaluyong City passed 'Resolution No. 396, S-1995' authorizing Mayor Benjamin Abalos to expropriate a 405 square meter parcel of land owned by the Heirs of Suguitan for the expansion of a public high school. The City filed an expropriation complaint. The heirs moved to dismiss, contending that under Section 19 of the Local Government Code of 1991, an LGU can exercise eminent domain only through an Ordinance, not a mere Resolution.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether a local government unit can validly file an expropriation complaint pursuant to a mere Sanggunian Resolution instead of an Ordinance.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Section 19 of the Local Government Code strictly mandates the enactment of an ORDINANCE to exercise eminent domain.

LEGAL BASIS Section 19 of Republic Act No. 7160 (The Local Government Code of 1991); Article III, Section 9 and Article X, Section 5 of the Constitution.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court ruled that local government units possess no inherent power of eminent domain; their power is purely delegated and must be exercised in strict conformity with the statutory grant. Section 19 of RA 7160 explicitly commands that an LGU may exercise eminent domain 'through its chief executive and acting pursuant to an ordinance.' An ordinance is a local law of permanent character passed with specific legislative formalities, whereas a resolution is a mere expression of opinion or temporary administrative intent. Filing an expropriation case based on a mere resolution is fatally defective and ultra vires.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, SET ASIDE the trial court order, and DISMISSED the expropriation complaint.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Eminent Domain Delegations) & Section 19 Local Government Code (Mandatory Ordinance Requirement) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Heirs of Suguitan is the strict statutory authority governing LGU expropriation: local governments must enact a formal Ordinance (never a mere Resolution) to lawfully commence eminent domain.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 43

SMI Development Corporation vs. Republic

CITATION 323 SCRA 862, G.R. No. 137537, January 28, 2000 | Supreme Court Third Division (Panganiban, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Republic filed a complaint for eminent domain to expropriate a lot owned by SMI Development Corporation in Pasig City for the expansion of the Department of Energy (DOE) office complex. SMI moved to dismiss, alleging that the Republic failed to state a definite, genuine public use and that the DOE had adequate alternative vacant lots within its existing compound.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the expropriation complaint sufficiently alleged a genuine public necessity and public use.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The expansion of government agency offices is a legitimate public use, and judicial inquiry into necessity is limited to checking fraud or bad faith.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 of the Constitution; Rule 67 of the Rules of Court; Concept of Public Use in Modern Eminent Domain.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the concept of 'public use' in eminent domain is no longer restricted to direct physical use by the entire public. It extends to any use which promotes government operational efficiency and public utility. Expropriating land for government department offices (DOE) is directly beneficial to public administration. The determination of the necessity for taking a particular parcel lies within legislative and executive discretion, which courts will not overturn absent clear showing of grave abuse of discretion, arbitrary action, or bad faith.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DENIED the petition and SUSTAINED the trial court's order denying the motion to dismiss.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Modern Broad Concept of Public Use & Judicial Review Limits) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: SMI Development confirms the expansive modern doctrine of 'Public Use': public administrative facilities satisfy the constitutional test of public use, and courts defer to executive necessity determinations unless taint of fraud or arbitrariness is proven.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 44

Philippine Press Institute vs. Commission on Elections

CITATION 244 SCRA 272, G.R. No. 119694, May 22, 1995 | Supreme Court En Banc (Feliciano, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

COMELEC issued Resolution No. 2772 mandating that all print media enterprises provide free 'Comelec Space' of not less than one-half page in three issues for political candidate advertisements. The Philippine Press Institute (PPI) challenged the resolution, arguing that compelling newspapers to provide free print space constituted an unconstitutional taking of private property without just compensation.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether compelling print media publishers to donate free advertising space to the government constitutes an unconstitutional taking of property under Eminent Domain.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Coercing print media to provide free advertising space is an unconstitutional taking of private property without just compensation.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 of the Constitution (Taking of Property & Just Compensation); Article III, Section 4 (Freedom of the Press).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that print media space is valuable private commercial property. While COMELEC has the constitutional power under Article IX-C, Section 4 to supervise media during elections, that power does not authorize the confiscation of private property without compensation. Unlike broadcast airwaves (which belong to the public domain and are operated under government franchise), the print press operates independently on private capital. Forcing newspapers to surrender commercial advertising space for free constitutes an impermissible taking under eminent domain without paying just compensation, while simultaneously infringing freedom of the press.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition and declared Section 2 of COMELEC Resolution No. 2772 UNCONSTITUTIONAL and VOID.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Compensable Taking vs. Police Regulation) & Article III, Section 4 (Press Freedom) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: PPI v. COMELEC draws the crucial constitutional line between police regulation and eminent domain: the State cannot compel private print publishers to surrender valuable commercial space for public election purposes without paying fair market compensation.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 45

TELEBAP, Inc. vs. Commission on Elections

CITATION 289 SCRA 337, G.R. No. 132922, April 21, 1998 | Supreme Court En Banc (Mendoza, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Section 92 of BP Blg. 881 (Omnibus Election Code) required radio and television stations to provide free airtime ('Comelec Time') to the Commission on Elections for candidate forums and voter education during election campaigns. The Telecommunications and Broadcast Attorneys of the Philippines (TELEBAP) and GMA Network challenged the statute, arguing that compelling broadcast stations to donate free airtime was a compensable taking violating the PPI v. COMELEC doctrine.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether requiring broadcast franchise holders to provide free 'Comelec Time' constitutes an unconstitutional taking of private property without just compensation.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Providing free airtime is a valid regulatory condition of a public broadcast franchise under Police Power, NOT an eminent domain taking.

LEGAL BASIS Article XII, Section 11 (Franchises Subject to Amendment & Public Interest); Article IX-C, Section 4 (COMELEC Media Supervision); Article III, Section 9.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court distinguished TELEBAP from PPI v. COMELEC. The electromagnetic airwaves used in broadcasting are part of the public domain. Broadcast stations operate pursuant to legislative franchises that are mere privileges granted by the State, subject to amendment, alteration, or conditions under Article XII, Section 11. In exchange for the exclusive privilege of utilizing public airwaves, the State may validly impose the public service condition of providing free airtime for election discussions. This is a legitimate exercise of police power and franchise regulation, not a taking of private property.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition and SUSTAINED the constitutionality of Section 92 of the Omnibus Election Code.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XII, Section 11 (Broadcast Franchise Conditions), Article IX-C, Section 4 (COMELEC Regulation), & Police Power Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: TELEBAP v. COMELEC establishes the 'Broadcast Spectrum Exception': because broadcast operators utilize public airwaves under state franchise, mandatory free campaign airtime is a valid franchise condition, unlike uncompensated takings of private print media.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 46

Heirs of Juancho Ardona vs. Reyes

CITATION 125 SCRA 220, G.R. Nos. L-60549-55, October 26, 1983 | Supreme Court En Banc (Gutierrez, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Philippine Tourism Authority (PTA) initiated expropriation proceedings against 282 hectares of land in Barangay Malubog and Babag, Cebu City, to construct a sports complex, golf course, hotel, and tourist resort under Presidential Decree No. 564. Landowners (Heirs of Ardona) challenged the taking, arguing that tourism development, recreation, and golf courses do not constitute 'public use' under Eminent Domain.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the expropriation of private agricultural land for tourism development and sports complex facilities constitutes a valid 'public use' under Eminent Domain.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Tourism development, economic growth, and recreational facilities fall squarely within the modern constitutional concept of 'Public Use.'

LEGAL BASIS Article IV, Section 2 of the 1973 Constitution (Article III, Section 9 of the 1987 Constitution); Presidential Decree No. 564; Broad Concept of Public Use.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the concept of 'public use' is dynamic and co-extensive with the growing needs of society. Public use is no longer confined to 'use by the public at large' (such as roads or bridges), but includes anything that tends to enlarge resources, generate employment, stimulate foreign exchange, and promote the general welfare of the community. Tourism development is a vital economic engine recognized by national policy. Consequently, expropriation for integrated tourism and sports facilities satisfies the constitutional public use requirement.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petitions and UPHELD the validity of the expropriation proceedings for the tourism complex.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Expanded Concept of Public Use for Economic Development) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Ardona v. Reyes is the landmark ruling cementing the modern expansive definition of 'Public Use' in Philippine Eminent Domain, confirming that tourism, economic infrastructure, and recreation serve constitutional public purposes.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 47

Office of the Solicitor General vs. Ayala Land Incorporated

CITATION 600 SCRA 617, G.R. No. 177056, September 18, 2009 | Supreme Court Third Division (Chico-Nazario, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Senate Committee on Trade and Commerce recommended that shopping malls (Ayala Land, Robinsons, SM Prime) be prohibited from charging parking fees to customers pursuant to the National Building Code (PD 1096). The OSG filed a petition to enjoin mall operators from collecting parking fees, arguing that the Building Code requires mall owners to provide parking spaces for free.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether prohibiting commercial shopping mall operators from charging parking fees constitutes an unconstitutional taking of private property without just compensation.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Prohibiting mall operators from charging parking fees is an unconstitutional taking of private property without just compensation.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 of the 1987 Constitution; Article III, Section 1 (Due Process Clause); Presidential Decree No. 1096 (National Building Code).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that while the National Building Code requires commercial establishments to provide parking spaces, nowhere in the statute is it mandated that such spaces must be provided free of charge. The construction and maintenance of multi-level parking structures require immense private capital investments, insurance, lighting, and security. Compelling private mall owners to provide free parking would totally deprive them of the economic use and fruits of their property, constituting a confiscatory taking under eminent domain disguised as police regulation without paying just compensation.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DENIED the OSG's petition and AFFIRMED the right of mall operators to charge reasonable parking fees.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Confiscatory Police Regulations as Unconstitutional Takings) & Article III, Section 1 (Substantive Due Process) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: OSG v. Ayala Land establishes that state regulations which deprive a property owner of all reasonable economic utility and fruits of private property cross the line into an unconstitutional taking under Eminent Domain.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 48

Mosqueda vs. Pilipino Banana Growers & Exporters Association, Inc.

CITATION 798 SCRA 160, G.R. No. 189185, August 16, 2016 | Supreme Court En Banc (Bersamin, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The City of Davao enacted City Ordinance No. 0309-07 banning aerial spraying of agricultural chemicals in all agricultural plantations within Davao City, and requiring a 30-meter buffer zone along plantation boundaries. Banana exporters (PBGEA) challenged the ordinance, arguing that the total ban was arbitrary, oppressive, lacked scientific basis, violated equal protection, and constituted a taking of private property without due process.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether Davao City Ordinance No. 0309-07 is a valid exercise of police power or an unreasonable, oppressive, and unconstitutional measure.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER The ordinance is UNCONSTITUTIONAL and VOID for violating due process, equal protection, and the limits of police power.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 1 of the Constitution (Due Process & Equal Protection); Two Requisites of Valid Police Power (Lawful Subject & Lawful Means).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court struck down the ordinance for failing both tests of police power: (1) The means employed were not reasonably necessary: the City failed to present scientific evidence proving that aerial spraying per se caused environmental or health degradation, whereas banning all aerial spraying immediately without reasonable transition threatened to destroy the banana industry; (2) The ordinance violated equal protection by singling out aerial spraying while permitting equally hazardous ground spraying methods; and (3) Imposing a mandatory 30-meter buffer zone inside private plantations without compensation constituted an unconstitutional taking of private property.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the CA decision declaring Davao City Ordinance No. 0309-07 UNCONSTITUTIONAL and VOID.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 1 (Substantive Due Process & Equal Protection in Police Power) & Article III, Section 9 (Buffer Zone Taking) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Mosqueda v. PBGEA is a landmark modern authority articulating the strict constitutional limits on Local Police Power: regulations must rest on sound factual/scientific foundations, observe equal protection, and avoid oppressive, confiscatory prohibitions.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 49

Secretary of the DPWH vs. Spouses Tecson

CITATION 756 SCRA 389, G.R. No. 179334, April 21, 2015 | Supreme Court En Banc (Peralta, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

In 1940, the Department of Public Works and Highways (DPWH) took a 7,268 square meter parcel of land owned by Spouses Tecson in Bulacan for the construction of the MacArthur Highway, without filing expropriation proceedings or paying compensation. In 1994 (54 years later), the Tecsons demanded payment. The trial court awarded just compensation based on the 1994 market value (P1,500/sqm). The DPWH contended that valuation must be reckoned from the date of taking in 1940 (P0.70/sqm).

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether just compensation in expropriation should be reckoned from the time of actual taking (1940) or from the time of judicial claim/filing (1994), and how interest is computed.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER Just compensation is computed based on the FAIR MARKET VALUE AT THE TIME OF TAKING (1940), plus LEGAL INTEREST from the date of taking until fully paid.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 of the Constitution; Rule 67, Section 4 of the Rules of Court; Doctrine of Time of Taking Valuation.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc resolved the long-standing doctrinal controversy regarding delayed expropriation claims. The Court held that just compensation must be reckoned from the date of actual physical taking (1940) because that is the moment when the owner was deprived of ownership and possession. To award compensation based on current inflated values would grant an unjust windfall resulting from government highway improvements. However, to compensate for the 54-year delay and monetary devaluation, the State must pay legal interest (6% to 12% per annum) running continuously from the date of taking in 1940 until full payment, along with exemplary damages and attorney's fees.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court MODIFIED the award: fixed base compensation at P0.70/sqm (1940 value) plus 6% interest per annum compounded until full payment, plus exemplary damages and attorney's fees.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Just Compensation: Time of Taking Rule & Accrual of Legal Interest) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: DPWH v. Spouses Tecson is the definitive ruling establishing that just compensation is pegged to fair market value at the time of taking, with compounded legal interest serving as the constitutional equalizer for government delay.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 50

National Power Corporation vs. Heirs of Macabangkit Sangkay

CITATION 656 SCRA 60, G.R. No. 165828, August 24, 2011 | Supreme Court First Division (Bersamin, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

In 1979, the National Power Corporation (NPC) constructed an underground water diversion tunnel for the Agus II Hydroelectric Project 100 meters beneath the ancestral land of the Heirs of Sangkay in Marawi City, without their knowledge or consent. In 1997, the heirs discovered the underground tunnel and filed a complaint for damages and inverse condemnation. NPC argued that the subterranean tunnel was not a taking and that the action had prescribed.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether the subterranean construction of a hydroelectric tunnel underneath private land constitutes a compensable taking under Eminent Domain. 2. Whether an action for inverse condemnation prescribes.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER 1. YES. Underground occupation is a compensable taking of property under Article 437 of the Civil Code and Eminent Domain. 2. NO. An action for just compensation against an illegal taking without expropriation does not prescribe.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 of the Constitution; Article 437 of the Civil Code (Subterranean Land Ownership); Doctrine of Inverse Condemnation.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that under Article 437 of the Civil Code, ownership of land extends not merely to the surface but to the subsoil beneath it. The construction of a massive underground hydroelectric tunnel effectively deprives the landowner of the full use and enjoyment of the subterranean property, constituting a permanent taking under eminent domain. Where the government takes property without filing formal expropriation proceedings, the aggrieved owner may institute an 'Inverse Condemnation' action to compel payment. Such an action does not prescribe because eminent domain taking without compensation is a continuing constitutional violation.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the decision holding NPC liable to pay just compensation with legal interest.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Inverse Condemnation & Subterranean Takings) & Article 437 Civil Code (Cujus est solum doctrine) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: NPC v. Sangkay establishes the Doctrine of Inverse Condemnation in Philippine law and confirms that underground subsoil occupation constitutes a permanent compensable taking.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 51

Export Processing Zone Authority (now PEZA) vs. Pulido

CITATION 656 SCRA 315, G.R. No. 177992, August 24, 2011 | Supreme Court Second Division (Carpio, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

EPZA expropriated lands in Cavite for export zone expansion. In an earlier appeal (EPZA v. San Antonio), the Supreme Court had already fixed the valuation of adjacent parcels within the same expropriated project at a specific rate per square meter. In subsequent proceedings involving respondent Pulido's adjacent lot, the trial court fixed a substantially higher valuation. PEZA invoked the Doctrine of the Law of the Case.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the prior Supreme Court ruling fixing land valuation in the same expropriation project constitutes the Law of the Case governing subsequent adjacent valuations.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Under the Law of the Case doctrine, judicial determinations on identical valuation issues in the same expropriation project are binding.

LEGAL BASIS Doctrine of the Law of the Case; Article III, Section 9 of the Constitution; Principle of Uniformity in Just Compensation.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the Law of the Case doctrine provides that whatever has been once irrevocably established as the controlling legal rule of decision between the same parties or involving the same expropriation project continues to be the law of the case. Where contiguous parcels sharing identical characteristics, zoning, and topography are expropriated simultaneously for a single public project, uniformity and fairness dictate that just compensation must be computed consistently with the valuation standard previously settled by the appellate court.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court REVERSED the higher award and FIXED just compensation in accordance with the established valuation in the related expropriation ruling.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Uniformity of Just Compensation) & Judicial Stability Doctrines Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: PEZA v. Pulido applies the Law of the Case doctrine to Eminent Domain, ensuring equality, stability, and uniformity in land valuation across contiguous lots in a unified public project.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 52

Republic vs. Judge Mupas and PIATCO

CITATION 769 SCRA 384, G.R. No. 181892, September 8, 2015 | Supreme Court En Banc (Brion, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following the nullification of the NAIA Terminal 3 build-operate-transfer contracts in Agan v. PIATCO, the Republic filed expropriation proceedings before the RTC of Pasay (under Judge Mupas) to take possession and ownership of the completed NAIA Terminal 3 facility. A major controversy arose regarding the valuation methodology for just compensation: whether to use the Income Approach / Equity Approach or the Depreciated Replacement Cost Method.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

What is the constitutionally sound valuation methodology for determining just compensation for the expropriation of a massive public infrastructure facility (NAIA Terminal 3).

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER The DEPRECIATED REPLACEMENT COST METHOD is the correct constitutional valuation standard for specialized public infrastructure improvements.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 of the Constitution; Republic Act No. 8974 (Right-of-Way Act); Principles of Just Compensation Valuation.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that just compensation is the full and fair equivalent of the property taken at the time of taking. Because NAIA 3 was a specialized airport terminal built under a nullified contract, income capitalization or commercial equity approaches were inapplicable as they would improperly compensate for anticipated future business profits. Instead, the Depreciated Replacement Cost (DRC) method - which computes the current cost of constructing an exact replica of the facility, minus structural and functional depreciation - represents the true objective value of the physical asset. The Court fixed the total just compensation at $519.8 million plus 6% legal interest.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court MODIFIED the RTC judgment and FIXED the final just compensation for NAIA Terminal 3 under the Depreciated Replacement Cost method.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Eminent Domain Valuation Methodology for Specialized Infrastructure) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Republic v. Mupas & PIATCO is the definitive jurisprudence establishing the Depreciated Replacement Cost (DRC) methodology for valuing specialized, non-market public infrastructure in Eminent Domain.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 53

Land Bank of the Philippines vs. Dalauta

CITATION 835 SCRA 268, G.R. No. 190004, August 8, 2017 | Supreme Court En Banc (Mendoza, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Respondent Herminio Dalauta owned 25 hectares of agricultural land in Butuan City placed under the Comprehensive Agrarian Reform Program (CARP). LBP and DAR offered P192,782 as just compensation pursuant to DAR Administrative Order No. 6. Dalauta rejected the valuation and filed an agrarian case before the Regional Trial Court sitting as a Special Agrarian Court (SAC). The SAC fixed just compensation at P2.6 million, disregarding the DAR formula.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the determination of just compensation in agrarian reform is an exclusive judicial function and how DAR administrative formulas should be applied.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The determination of just compensation is an EXCLUSIVE JUDICIAL FUNCTION; DAR administrative formulas serve as mandatory baseline guidelines.

LEGAL BASIS Article III, Section 9 and Article XIII, Section 4 of the Constitution; Section 17 and Section 57 of Republic Act No. 6657 (CARP Law); Export Processing Zone Authority v. Dulay (149 SCRA 305).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that under the Constitution and the landmark Dulay doctrine, the determination of just compensation is fundamentally and exclusively a judicial prerogative. Executive bodies and administrative agencies (like DAR and LBP) can only make preliminary administrative determinations. However, in exercising this judicial power, Special Agrarian Courts are legally required to consider the statutory factors under Section 17 of RA 6657 and the mathematical formulas prescribed by DAR AO. If a SAC deviates from the DAR formula, it must articulate reasoned, evidence-based justifications for the departure.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court REMANDED the case to the Special Agrarian Court for re-computation of just compensation strictly applying the statutory criteria and DAR formulas.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Exclusive Judicial Power to Fix Just Compensation) & Article XIII, Section 4 (Agrarian Reform Compensation) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: LBP v. Dalauta synthesizes the twin pillars of agrarian eminent domain: the exclusive judicial power to determine just compensation and the mandatory baseline application of DAR statutory formulas.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 54

Alfonso vs. Land Bank of the Philippines and DAR

CITATION 809 SCRA 624, G.R. Nos. 181912 & 183347, November 29, 2016 | Supreme Court En Banc (Jardeleza, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Petitioner Remedios Alfonso owned agricultural land in Pandi, Bulacan, expropriated under CARP. LBP valued the land at P3.1 million using DAR Administrative Order No. 5. Alfonso filed a petition with the SAC, which fixed compensation at P5.6 million without using the DAR formula, relying solely on prevailing zonal market values. The Court of Appeals reversed and remanded, prompting the appeal to the Supreme Court En Banc.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether Special Agrarian Courts (SACs) are legally bound to apply the DAR formulas in calculating just compensation for lands acquired under RA 6657.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. SACs must apply the DAR formulas; any deviation must be explicitly justified by specific evidence on record.

LEGAL BASIS Section 17 of Republic Act No. 6657; Article III, Section 9 and Article XIII, Section 4 of the 1987 Constitution.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc affirmed that while fixing just compensation is a judicial function, the Legislature has the constitutional authority to prescribe statutory factors (such as capitalized net income, comparable sales, and market value). The DAR administrative formulas are the statutory translation of Section 17 of RA 6657. Therefore, trial courts cannot arbitrarily set aside DAR formulas to rely exclusively on zonal or market values. If the facts of a specific case justify a departure from the formula, the SAC must clearly explain the factual and evidentiary reasons for doing so.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court AFFIRMED the CA decision remanding the case to the SAC for proper determination of just compensation in accordance with the DAR formulas.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 9 (Just Compensation Standards) & Article XIII, Section 4 (Agrarian Reform Law Standards) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Alfonso v. LBP solidifies the rule that trial courts cannot disregard statutory agrarian reform valuation formulas on a whim: adherence to Section 17 factors and DAR formulas is mandatory unless proven inadequate.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

TOPIC IV: STATE POLICIES, PRINCIPLES AND GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS

Republicanism, Separation of Powers, Ecology, Nationalist Provisions, Regalian Doctrine

★ TOPIC IV • KEY CONSTITUTIONAL DOCTRINES & LECTURE OUTLINE • Continuing Mandamus: Judicial oversight enforcing mandatory ministerial duties to protect ecology without violating separation of powers (MMDA v. Manila Bay). • Pork Barrel Unconstitutionality: Non-delegation and separation of powers bar post-enactment legislative project control and preserve the item-veto (Belgica v. Ochoa). • Intergenerational Responsibility: Self-executing right to a balanced ecology confers standing to sue on behalf of future generations (Oposa v. Factoran, Resident Marine Mammals). • Institutional Academic Freedom: Four essential freedoms empower educational and military academies to enforce ethical standards (Cadet Cudia v. PMA). • Nationalist Economic Provisions: 60-40 Filipino ownership requires voting control and beneficial capital (Roy III v. Herbosa); foreign land purchase options are void ab initio (PNOC v. Keppel).
CASE 55

MMDA vs. Concerned Residents of Manila Bay

CITATION 643 SCRA 90, G.R. Nos. 171947-48, February 15, 2011 | Supreme Court En Banc (Velasco, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Fourteen young Filipinos (Concerned Residents of Manila Bay) filed a complaint for cleanup and rehabilitation of Manila Bay against various government agencies (MMDA, DENR, DPWH, DOH, DepEd, DA, BFAR, Philippine Ports Authority, PNP Maritime Group, and MWSS). The RTC and CA ordered the agencies to clean, rehabilitate, and restore Manila Bay waters to Class SB classification (fit for swimming and recreation). MMDA and other agencies appealed, arguing that cleaning the bay was not a ministerial duty and that ongoing judicial supervision violated the Separation of Powers.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the Supreme Court may issue a Writ of Continuing Mandamus directing executive agencies to perform their statutory duties to clean Manila Bay without violating Separation of Powers.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The issuance of a Writ of Continuing Mandamus is a valid exercise of judicial power to enforce ministerial environmental duties.

LEGAL BASIS Article II, Section 16 of the 1987 Constitution ('The State shall protect and advance the right of the people to a balanced and healthful ecology'); Republic Act No. 9275 (Clean Water Act); Presidential Decree No. 1152 (Philippine Environment Code); Rule 8, Rules of Procedure for Environmental Cases.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the duty of executive agencies to clean, rehabilitate, and protect the waters of Manila Bay is a general and continuous ministerial mandate imposed by statutory environmental laws (Environment Code, Clean Water Act, and Local Government Code). It is not discretionary. Because of the extreme degradation of Manila Bay and decades of bureaucratic neglect, the Court fashioned the extraordinary remedy of 'Continuing Mandamus.' Under Continuing Mandamus, the Court retains jurisdiction and requires periodic compliance reports from the agencies until the mandate is completely fulfilled. This does not violate Separation of Powers because the Court does not usurp administrative discretion, but merely compels agencies to perform their clear statutory obligations.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court ENJOINED all respondent agencies to continuously execute the Operational Plan for the Manila Bay Coastal Strategy and submit quarterly compliance reports.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article II, Section 16 (Right to Ecology), Article VIII, Section 1 (Expanded Judicial Power), & Principle of Separation of Powers Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: MMDA v. Concerned Residents of Manila Bay is the definitive landmark on the Writ of Continuing Mandamus in environmental constitutional law, harmonizing judicial oversight with executive enforcement to realize the constitutional right to ecology.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 56

Belgica vs. Ochoa, Jr. (The PDAF Case)

CITATION 710 SCRA 1, G.R. No. 208566, November 19, 2013 | Supreme Court En Banc (Perlas-Bernabe, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following the whistleblowing revelations of the multi-billion-peso Janet Lim-Napoles pork barrel scam, Greco Belgica and other citizens filed petitions challenging the constitutionality of the Priority Development Assistance Fund (PDAF) provisions in the 2013 General Appropriations Act (GAA) and similar executive discretionary lump-sum funds (such as the Malampaya Fund under PD 910 and Presidential Social Fund under PD 1869).

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether the PDAF / Congressional Pork Barrel system violates the constitutional principles of Separation of Powers and Non-Delegation of Legislative Power. 2. Whether the PDAF violates the Constitutional System of Checks and Balances and Local Autonomy.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The PDAF / Pork Barrel system is UNCONSTITUTIONAL and VOID for violating Separation of Powers, Non-Delegation, Checks and Balances, and Local Autonomy.

LEGAL BASIS Article VI, Section 1 (Legislative Power & Non-Delegation); Article VI, Section 27(2) (Presidential Item-Veto Power); Article VI, Section 29(1) (Appropriations); Article X (Local Autonomy); Two Tests of Delegation (Completeness Test & Sufficient Standard Test).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that the legislative power of Congress is complete upon the enactment of the General Appropriations Act. Once the budget law is enacted, the power to execute the budget belongs exclusively to the Executive. The PDAF system allowed individual legislators to perform post-enactment project identification, fund realignment, and project intervention, which constitutes an unconstitutional post-enactment legislative veto and encroachment into executive execution. Furthermore, lump-sum discretionary funds prevented the President from exercising the constitutional item-veto power under Article VI, Section 27(2), and bypassed local development councils in violation of Local Autonomy.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court declared the 2013 PDAF Article and all post-enactment legislative budget intervention mechanisms UNCONSTITUTIONAL and VOID.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VI, Section 1 (Separation of Powers & Non-Delegation), Article VI, Section 27(2) (Item-Veto), & Article X, Section 3 (Local Autonomy) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Belgica v. Ochoa is the monumental modern landmark that dismantled the Pork Barrel system in the Philippines, strictly enforcing the principle that legislators cannot exercise post-enactment executive power or subvert the presidential item-veto.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 57

Gonzales III vs. Office of the President

CITATION 714 SCRA 611, G.R. No. 196231, January 28, 2014 | Supreme Court En Banc (Brion, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following the 2010 Manila Hostage Crisis (Luneta Grandstand), the Office of the President (OP) initiated administrative disciplinary proceedings against Deputy Ombudsman Emilio Gonzales III pursuant to Section 8(2) of Republic Act No. 6770 (The Ombudsman Act of 1989), which authorized the President to remove a Deputy Ombudsman or Special Prosecutor. The OP found Gonzales guilty of gross neglect of duty and ordered his dismissal. Gonzales challenged the constitutionality of Section 8(2) of RA 6770.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether Section 8(2) of RA 6770, which vests the President with administrative disciplinary authority over the Deputy Ombudsman, is unconstitutional for violating the Constitutional Independence of the Ombudsman.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Subjecting the Deputy Ombudsman to the disciplinary power of the President destroys the Constitutional Independence of the Office of the Ombudsman.

LEGAL BASIS Article XI, Section 5 of the 1987 Constitution ('There is hereby created the independent Office of the Ombudsman'); Article XI, Section 8, 9, 12, and 13; Principle of Constitutional Independence.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that the 1987 Constitution intentionally created the Office of the Ombudsman as a fiercely independent constitutional watchdog to insulate it from political pressures and executive influence. The Ombudsman performs oversight over all government officials, including the Executive. To grant the President the power to discipline and remove the Deputy Ombudsman - who exercises core constitutional investigatory powers - places a sword of Damocles over the Ombudsman's office, severely compromising its constitutional independence. Section 8(2) of RA 6770 was declared unconstitutional as applied to the Deputy Ombudsman.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court declared Section 8(2) of RA 6770 UNCONSTITUTIONAL and SET ASIDE the OP dismissal order against Deputy Ombudsman Gonzales.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XI, Section 5 (Independence of the Office of the Ombudsman) & Article XI, Section 12/13 (Powers & Protections) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Gonzales III v. OP establishes the constitutional safeguard of the Ombudsman's institutional independence: the President cannot possess disciplinary control over Deputy Ombudsmen whose duty is to impartially investigate executive malfeasance.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 58

Garcia vs. Executive Secretary

CITATION 677 SCRA 750, G.R. No. 198554, July 30, 2012 | Supreme Court En Banc (Perez, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Major General Carlos F. Garcia, former Comptroller of the Armed Forces of the Philippines (AFP), was charged before a General Court-Martial with violations of the Articles of War (conduct unbecoming an officer and gentleman, and failure to declare assets). The General Court-Martial convicted Garcia and sentenced him to two years hard labor, dishonorable discharge, and forfeiture of all pay. President Benigno Aquino III, as Commander-in-Chief, confirmed the sentence. Garcia filed a petition for certiorari and habeas corpus, arguing that the military confirmation process violated civilian supremacy and due process.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the military court-martial conviction and presidential confirmation violate civilian supremacy and the constitutional rights of an accused military officer.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Courts-martial are specialized military tribunals operating under the ultimate constitutional authority of the Civilian President as Commander-in-Chief.

LEGAL BASIS Article II, Section 3 of the 1987 Constitution ('Civilian authority is, at all times, supreme over the military'); Article VII, Section 18 (Commander-in-Chief Clause); Commonwealth Act No. 408 (Articles of War).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that military law is a separate jurisprudence designed to maintain strict discipline in the armed services. Courts-martial are executive agencies belonging to the Executive Department, not the Judicial branch. Under the Articles of War and Article VII, Section 18, the civilian President of the Philippines is the Commander-in-Chief of all armed forces. The President's review and confirmation of court-martial verdicts is the direct embodiment of Civilian Supremacy over the military, ensuring that military justice remains strictly subordinate to the civilian head of state.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED Garcia's petition and AFFIRMED the presidential confirmation of the court-martial conviction.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article II, Section 3 (Civilian Supremacy over the Military) & Article VII, Section 18 (Commander-in-Chief Powers) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Garcia v. Executive Secretary demonstrates the practical application of the Civilian Supremacy clause: the civilian President retains supreme disciplinary command over all military tribunals and armed forces personnel.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 59

Oposa vs. Factoran, Jr.

CITATION 224 SCRA 792, G.R. No. 101083, July 30, 1993 | Supreme Court En Banc (Davide, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

A group of Filipino minors, represented by their parents and the Philippine Ecological Network, Inc., filed a taxpayers' and class action against DENR Secretary Fulgencio S. Factoran, Jr., seeking the cancellation of all existing Timber License Agreements (TLAs) in the country and an order enjoining the issuance of new ones. Petitioners claimed they represented not only themselves but also generations yet unborn. The RTC dismissed the complaint for lack of cause of action and on the ground of non-impairment of contracts.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether the minors have legal standing to sue on behalf of future generations (Doctrine of Intergenerational Responsibility). 2. Whether the constitutional right to a balanced and healthful ecology (Article II, Section 16) is a self-executing, actionable right. 3. Whether timber licenses are protected contracts against non-impairment.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):
