II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether local legislative bodies (Sangguniang Panlungsod) possess the inherent power to cite and punish witnesses for contempt in legislative investigations.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Local legislative councils (Sanggunian) do NOT possess inherent contempt powers in the absence of an express statutory grant by Congress.

LEGAL BASIS Article X, Section 1 and Section 5 (Local Autonomy & Delegated Powers); Batas Pambansa Blg. 337 (Local Government Code of 1983); Distinction between National Congress and Local Councils.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the inherent contempt power recognized in Arnault v. Nazareno belongs exclusively to the sovereign national Legislature (Congress). Local legislative bodies (Sanggunians) are creatures of statute with strictly delegated powers. There is no statutory provision in the Local Government Code granting local councils the power to punish for contempt or issue arrest warrants. If a local council requires compulsory attendance of witnesses or contempt sanctions, it must apply to the regular courts (Regional Trial Court) for judicial enforcement.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, declared the Sanggunian contempt and arrest order NULL and VOID, and ENJOINED its enforcement.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article X, Section 1 (Delegated Status of LGUs) & Article VI, Section 21 (National Legislative Contempt vs Local Inability) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: NORECO v. Sangguniang Panlungsod establishes the strict constitutional rule that contempt powers are inherent only in the national Congress: local councils cannot jail citizens for contempt without express statutory authorization.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 89

David vs. Macapagal-Arroyo

CITATION 489 SCRA 160, G.R. No. 171396, May 3, 2006 | Supreme Court En Banc (Sandoval-Gutierrez, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

On February 24, 2006, President Gloria Macapagal-Arroyo issued Presidential Proclamation No. 1017 declaring a State of National Emergency and General Order No. 5 directing the AFP and PNP to prevent acts of terrorism and lawless violence. Under PP 1017, police raided The Daily Tribune newspaper offices, arrested Professor Randy David and Atty. Ronald Llamas without a warrant during a rally, and revoked public assembly permits. Multiple petitions challenged the constitutionality of PP 1017 and GO No. 5.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether the President is immune from suit during incumbency. 2. Whether Presidential Proclamation No. 1017 and General Order No. 5 are constitutional.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER 1. YES. An incumbent President enjoys absolute immunity from suit during tenure. 2. PP 1017 is VALID as a calling-out proclamation, but its provisions authorizing warrantless arrests, media censorship, and takeover of private enterprises are UNCONSTITUTIONAL.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 18 (Calling-Out Power); Article VI, Section 23(2) (Emergency Powers require statutory delegation by Congress); Article XII, Section 17 (Takeover of Public Utilities requires legislative delegation); Article III, Section 1, 4, and 6.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court dropped President Arroyo as a direct respondent based on presidential immunity from suit during incumbency. On the merits, the Court ruled that under the Calling-Out power, the President may lawfully direct the military to suppress lawless violence. However, the President cannot declare emergency powers, take over private businesses under Article XII, Section 17, or issue decrees without an emergency powers statute enacted by Congress under Article VI, Section 23(2). Warrantless arrests of peaceful protesters, censorship of media, and raids on newspapers violate the Bill of Rights.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court declared PP 1017 CONSTITUTIONAL in part (as a calling-out order), but UNCONSTITUTIONAL as to the takeover of businesses, warrantless arrests, and media raids.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VI, Section 23(2) (Delegated Emergency Powers), Article XII, Section 17 (Emergency Takeovers), & Article VII, Section 18 Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: David v. Macapagal-Arroyo is the monumental ruling defining the limits of Executive Emergency Powers: the President cannot assume legislative emergency powers or seize private businesses without express congressional delegation.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 90

Lecaroz vs. Sandiganbayan

CITATION 128 SCRA 324, G.R. No. L-56384, March 22, 1984 | Supreme Court En Banc (Relova, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Francisco Lecaroz, Mayor of Santa Cruz, Marinduque, was charged with usurpation of public authority before the Sandiganbayan. Lecaroz challenged the jurisdiction of the Sandiganbayan over an impeachable officer (or public officials generally), raising the doctrinal issue of whether an impeachable constitutional officer can be criminally charged in regular courts during incumbency.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether constitutional impeachable officers may be prosecuted criminally before the Sandiganbayan or regular courts prior to their removal via impeachment.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Impeachable constitutional officers cannot be criminally charged before courts for offenses warranting removal until removed via Impeachment.

LEGAL BASIS Article XI, Section 2 and Section 3(7) of the Constitution ('Judgment in cases of impeachment shall not extend further than removal from office and disqualification... but the party convicted shall nevertheless be liable and subject to prosecution, trial, and punishment according to law').

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the constitutional mechanism of impeachment is the exclusive method established by the fundamental law for removing high constitutional officers (the President, Vice-President, Members of the Supreme Court, Members of Constitutional Commissions, and Ombudsman). To permit lower courts or prosecutors to criminally charge and arrest an incumbent impeachable officer while in office would circumvent the impeachment process, strip the officer of constitutional independence, and disrupt vital state functions. An impeachable officer must first be removed through the constitutional impeachment process before criminal prosecution may proceed.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court REAFFIRMED the exclusive primacy of the impeachment process over criminal prosecution of impeachable officers during tenure.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XI, Section 2 (Impeachable Officers) & Article XI, Section 3(7) (Post-Impeachment Criminal Liability) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Lecaroz v. Sandiganbayan establishes the fundamental rule of Impeachment Primacy: high constitutional officers enjoy jurisdictional immunity from criminal prosecution during their incumbency until lawfully removed via impeachment.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 91

RE: EM No. 03-010 (COMELEC Order)

CITATION A.M. No. 03-8-22-SC, September 16, 2003 | Supreme Court En Banc (Panganiban, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Supreme Court was asked to clarify whether Constitutional Commissioners (such as COMELEC Commissioners) can be subjected to administrative disciplinary investigations or sanctions by any body other than the Congress through the Impeachment process.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether Members of the Constitutional Commissions (COMELEC, CSC, COA) can be administratively disciplined or removed by any authority other than Congress via Impeachment.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Constitutional Commissioners are impeachable officers and can be disciplined or removed ONLY through Impeachment by Congress.

LEGAL BASIS Article XI, Section 2 of the 1987 Constitution ('The President, the Vice-President, the Members of the Supreme Court, the Members of the Constitutional Commissions, and the Ombudsman may be removed from office on impeachment'); Article IX-A, Section 1.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc ruled that the Constitutional Commissions are independent constitutional bodies created by the Constitution itself. Under Article XI, Section 2, the commissioners are explicitly designated as impeachable officials. Their independence would be fatally compromised if they were made subject to administrative investigation, supervision, or disciplinary sanctions by the President, the Ombudsman, or internal administrative tribunals. The sole constitutional remedy for misfeasance, culpable violation of the Constitution, or graft by a constitutional commissioner is Impeachment by the House and trial by the Senate.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court HELD that no administrative disciplinary proceedings can be entertained against Constitutional Commissioners outside the Impeachment framework.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article XI, Section 2 (Exclusivity of Impeachment for Constitutional Commissioners) & Article IX-A (Independence Safeguards) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Re: EM No. 03-010 reinforces the absolute exclusivity of the Impeachment mechanism to protect the structural independence of the Constitutional Commissions from external administrative intimidation.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 92

Republic vs. Sereno

CITATION 863 SCRA 442, G.R. No. 237428, May 11, 2018 / June 19, 2018 | Supreme Court En Banc (Tijam, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Solicitor General filed a petition for Quo Warranto under Rule 66 against Chief Justice Maria Lourdes P. A. Sereno, seeking to oust her from the Office of the Chief Justice on the ground that she lacked the constitutional qualification of 'proven integrity' under Article VIII, Section 7(3) due to her repeated failure to file her Statements of Assets, Liabilities, and Net Worth (SALNs) with the JBC. Sereno argued that as an impeachable officer, she could be removed exclusively through Impeachment under Article XI, Section 2, and that the Supreme Court had no jurisdiction in quo warranto.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether a Quo Warranto petition under Rule 66 is a constitutionally permissible remedy to oust an incumbent impeachable officer (Chief Justice). 2. Whether Impeachment and Quo Warranto are mutually exclusive remedies.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER 1. YES. Quo Warranto is a valid, constitutionally permissible remedy to test the validity of appointment and eligibility at inception. 2. NO. Quo Warranto and Impeachment are distinct remedies that can proceed independently.

LEGAL BASIS Article VIII, Section 7(3) (Proven Integrity Qualification); Article VIII, Section 5(1) (Supreme Court Original Jurisdiction in Quo Warranto); Article XI, Section 2 (Impeachment); Rule 66 of the Rules of Court.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that Impeachment and Quo Warranto address fundamentally different legal situations. Impeachment is a political process that addresses offenses committed during incumbency to remove a lawfully appointed officer. Quo Warranto is a judicial proceeding that tests the title, eligibility, and qualifications of the appointee at the very time of appointment (ab initio). Because Sereno failed to comply with the mandatory SALN requirements of the JBC, she never acquired the constitutional qualification of proven integrity. Her appointment was void ab initio, meaning she was never a de jure Chief Justice. The Court has the constitutional power under Article VIII, Section 5(1) to issue writs of quo warranto against usurpation of public office.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition for Quo Warranto, declared Sereno's appointment NULL and VOID ab initio, and OUSTED her from the Office of the Chief Justice.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VIII, Section 5(1) (Quo Warranto Jurisdiction), Article VIII, Section 7(3) (Integrity Qualifications), & Article XI, Section 2 (Impeachment vs Quo Warranto) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Republic v. Sereno is the historic landmark defining the boundary between Impeachment (removal for offenses during incumbency) and Quo Warranto (nullification of title for lack of constitutional qualifications at inception).

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

TOPIC VI: EXECUTIVE DEPARTMENT

The Presidency, Succession, Control, Appointments, Military, and Clemency

★ TOPIC VI • KEY CONSTITUTIONAL DOCTRINES & LECTURE OUTLINE • Constructive Resignation & Immunity Limits: Permanent vacancy arises upon totality of relinquishment acts; immunity ends upon leaving office (Estrada v. Desierto). • Strict Multiple Office Ban: Cabinet members are strictly prohibited from holding other offices except uncompensated ex-officio posts germane to their departments (CLU v. Executive Secretary). • Residual Executive Powers: The President possesses plenary inherent powers to preserve peace and order (Marcos v. Manglapus). • Midnight Appointments Ban: Strict 4-requisite rule; ban does not apply to Supreme Court appointments (De Castro v. JBC, Velicaria-Garafil, Valenzuela). • Commander-in-Chief Powers & Open Court Doctrine: Calling-out power belongs solely to the President (Kulayan v. Tan); military commissions have no jurisdiction over civilians when civil courts function (Olaguer). • Constitutional Clemency & DAP Limits: Pardon remits penalties prospectively but does not erase guilt without proof of innocence (Monsanto, Garcia); cross-border savings transfers violate the Constitution (Araullo v. Aquino).
CASE 93

Estrada vs. Desierto

CITATION 353 SCRA 452, G.R. Nos. 146710-15, March 2, 2001 | Supreme Court En Banc (Puno, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following the collapse of his impeachment trial and the massive popular uprising known as EDSA People Power II, President Joseph Ejercito Estrada vacated Malacañang Palace on January 20, 2001. Vice-President Gloria Macapagal-Arroyo took her oath as President before Chief Justice Hilario Davide, Jr. Estrada subsequently filed petitions before the Supreme Court claiming he was merely a President on leave under Article VII, Section 11, that Arroyo was merely an Acting President, and that as incumbent President he enjoyed immunity from criminal prosecution for plunder.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether President Estrada resigned as President, creating a permanent vacancy under Article VII, Section 8. 2. Whether a former President enjoys immunity from criminal prosecution for acts committed while in office.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER 1. YES. Estrada constructively resigned from the presidency based on the totality of prior, contemporaneous, and subsequent acts. 2. NO. Presidential immunity from suit is co-extensive with tenure and ceases upon departure from office.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 8 (Presidential Vacancy and Succession); Article VII, Section 11 (Temporary Disability); Doctrine of Constructive Resignation; Presidential Immunity Limits.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that resignation does not require a specific formal ritual; it requires: (1) an intent to resign (animus desinendi), and (2) an act of relinquishment. Applying the 'Totality of Acts Test' (the Angara Diary accounts of negotiations, Estrada's press statements expressing gratitude to the people, and his physical departure from Malacañang without asserting presidential authority), the Court concluded that Estrada constructively resigned on January 20, 2001. Consequently, Vice-President Arroyo succeeded as the de jure 14th President under Article VII, Section 8. Furthermore, presidential immunity from suit is an official privilege enjoyed only during incumbency to prevent distraction from state duties. Once a President leaves office, immunity vanishes and does not shield criminal offenses.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED Estrada's petitions and declared Arroyo the lawful de jure President.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 8 (Presidential Succession & Resignation) & Article VII, Section 1 (Executive Power & Immunity Bounds) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Estrada v. Desierto is the leading authority on Constructive Resignation and Presidential Immunity: it confirms that immunity is strictly confined to tenure and does not protect non-incumbents from criminal prosecution.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 94

Civil Liberties Union vs. Executive Secretary

CITATION 194 SCRA 317, G.R. No. 83896, February 22, 1991 | Supreme Court En Banc (Fernan, C.J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

President Corazon C. Aquino issued Executive Order No. 284 allowing Cabinet members, undersecretaries, and assistant secretaries to hold up to two (2) other government positions in addition to their primary posts, with entitlement to receive additional compensation. The Civil Liberties Union (CLU) and Anti-Graft League challenged EO 284 as a direct violation of the strict constitutional ban against multiple offices under Article VII, Section 13 of the 1987 Constitution.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether Executive Order No. 284 is unconstitutional for violating the strict prohibition on holding multiple offices under Article VII, Section 13.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Executive Order No. 284 is UNCONSTITUTIONAL and VOID for violating Article VII, Section 13.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 13 of the 1987 Constitution ('The President, Vice-President, the Members of the Cabinet, and their deputies or assistants shall not, unless otherwise provided in this Constitution, hold any other office or employment during their tenure'); Article IX-B, Section 8 (General Civil Service Rule).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that Article VII, Section 13 imposes an absolute, strict, and uncompromising prohibition against Cabinet members holding multiple offices. The general exception in Article IX-B, Section 8 ('unless otherwise allowed by law') does NOT apply to Cabinet members, who are governed by the much stricter special rule of Article VII, Section 13 ('unless otherwise provided in this Constitution'). The only constitutional exception is when the Constitution itself specifically authorizes it (e.g., Vice-President appointed to the Cabinet under Art. VII, Sec. 3; Secretary of Justice sitting ex-officio in the JBC under Art. VIII, Sec. 8). EO 284, being a mere executive issuance, could not create statutory exceptions to a strict constitutional ban.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition and declared Executive Order No. 284 UNCONSTITUTIONAL and NULL and VOID.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 13 (Strict Ban on Multiple Offices for Cabinet) vs. Article IX-B, Section 8 (General Civil Service Rule) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: CLU v. Executive Secretary is the cornerstone precedent enforcing the strict constitutional ban on multiple offices for Cabinet members, preventing conflicts of interest and concentration of executive power.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 95

Clarificatory Resolution in CLU & Anti-Graft League vs. Juico

CITATION G.R. No. 83896, August 1, 1991 | Supreme Court En Banc (Fernan, C.J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following the nullification of EO 284 in Civil Liberties Union v. Executive Secretary, the Executive Department sought clarification regarding the status of Cabinet members serving in ex-officio capacities on boards of state agencies and corporations attached to their departments (e.g., Secretary of Agriculture sitting as ex-officio Chairman of the National Food Authority or Land Bank).

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the constitutional ban in Article VII, Section 13 prohibits Cabinet members from holding ex-officio positions closely related to their primary functions.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Holding EX-OFFICIO positions mandated by law that are closely related to the primary functions of the department and without additional compensation is permissible.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 13; Doctrine of Ex-Officio Functions; Administrative Code of 1987.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court clarified that the prohibition under Article VII, Section 13 does not apply to ex-officio designations. An ex-officio position is not an independent 'other office,' but an inherent adjunct and extension of the principal office created by legislative mandate. To be valid: (1) The ex-officio post must be closely related and germane to the primary functions of the Cabinet department, and (2) The official must serve WITHOUT receiving any additional salary, per diem, allowances, or compensation. Monies received in violation of this rule must be refunded to the national treasury.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court CLARIFIED that ex-officio posts strictly meeting the two criteria are valid, while reiterating the ban on additional compensation.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 13 (Ex-Officio Exception & Prohibition on Double Compensation) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: The Juico Clarificatory Resolution established the 'Ex-Officio Doctrine': Cabinet officials may sit on attached agency boards only if the duties are germane to their department and performed without additional pay.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 96

Public Interest Center Inc. vs. Elma

CITATION 494 SCRA 53, G.R. No. 138965, June 30, 2006 | Supreme Court En Banc (Chico-Nazario, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

President Joseph Estrada appointed Atty. Magdangal B. Elma as Chief Presidential Legal Counsel (CPLC), and subsequently appointed him concurrently as Chairman of the Presidential Commission on Good Government (PCGG). Petitioner Public Interest Center challenged the concurrent appointment, arguing it violated Article VII, Section 13.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the concurrent appointment of one individual as Chief Presidential Legal Counsel (CPLC) and PCGG Chairman violates Article VII, Section 13.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The concurrent appointment violates Article VII, Section 13 because PCGG Chairman is an independent public office and not an ex-officio post of the CPLC.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 13 of the 1987 Constitution; Civil Liberties Union v. Executive Secretary; Executive Order No. 1 (Creating PCGG).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that the CPLC and the PCGG Chairman are two separate, distinct, and incompatible public offices. The CPLC is an advisory officer providing legal advice directly to the President. The PCGG is a specialized quasi-judicial and investigative body tasked with recovering ill-gotten wealth, possessing powers to issue sequestration orders and file cases before the Sandiganbayan. The chairmanship of the PCGG is not an ex-officio attachment to the CPLC. Holding both offices concurrently concentrates incompatible powers in one person, in direct defiance of the strict constitutional prohibition in Article VII, Section 13.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition and declared the concurrent appointment of respondent Elma as PCGG Chairman and CPLC UNCONSTITUTIONAL and VOID.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 13 (Ban on Holding Multiple Incompatible Offices in the Executive) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Public Interest Center v. Elma strictly applies the CLU doctrine, confirming that executive officials cannot hold concurrent distinct administrative offices unless explicitly authorized by the Constitution.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 97

Soliven vs. Makasiar / Beltran vs. Makasiar

CITATION 167 SCRA 393, G.R. Nos. 82585, 82827 & 86250, November 14, 1988 | Supreme Court En Banc (Per Curiam)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Journalist Luis Beltran published a column in the Philippine Star alleging that during the December 1987 coup attempt, President Corazon C. Aquino 'hid under her bed.' President Aquino personally filed a criminal complaint for libel against Beltran and Maximo Soliven. Beltran argued that because an incumbent President is immune from suit, the President cannot file criminal complaints against citizens without violating equal protection.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether presidential immunity from suit prevents an incumbent President from filing a criminal complaint for libel against a private citizen.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Presidential immunity is a personal privilege of the President that shields the Chief Executive from being sued; it does NOT disable the President from initiating suits.

LEGAL BASIS Doctrine of Presidential Immunity from Suit; Article VII, Section 1; Article III, Section 1 (Equal Protection of Laws).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that presidential immunity from suit is a personal privilege recognized in the President to ensure that the Chief Executive can perform high sovereign functions without being harassed or distracted by continuous court litigation. The privilege belongs to the President and may be waived by the President. Immunity is a shield against being sued as a defendant; it does not prevent the President from seeking judicial redress as an injured complainant in criminal proceedings. Invoking the protection of penal laws does not violate equal protection.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED Beltran's petition and UPHELD the validity of the criminal libel proceedings initiated by President Aquino.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 1 (Nature and Scope of Presidential Immunity from Suit) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Soliven v. Makasiar is the foundational authority clarifying that presidential immunity is a defensive shield for the President, which the President may choose to waive to institute legal actions.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 98

Marcos vs. Manglapus

CITATION 177 SCRA 668, G.R. No. 88211, September 15, 1989 | Supreme Court En Banc (Cortés, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following the 1986 EDSA Revolution, former President Ferdinand E. Marcos and his family lived in exile in Hawaii. In 1989, facing impending death, Marcos sought to return to the Philippines. President Corazon C. Aquino, acting through DFA Secretary Raul Manglapus, prohibited their return on national security grounds. The Marcos family filed a petition for mandamus and prohibition, invoking their constitutional right to travel (Article III, Section 6).

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the President has the constitutional and residual power under Article VII, Section 1 to bar the return of a deposed dictator to protect national security and public peace.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The President possesses RESIDUAL POWERS under Article VII, Section 1 to protect the State and preserve peace and order.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 1 of the 1987 Constitution ('The executive power shall be vested in the President of the Philippines'); Article II, Section 4 & 5 (Duty of Government to protect the People); Residual Executive Powers.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that executive power is not confined to the specific enumerated powers explicitly cataloged in the text of Article VII. Executive power is plenary and includes all unstated, inherent 'Residual Powers' necessary for the President to fulfill the constitutional duty to preserve and protect the safety, tranquility, and democratic stability of the Republic. Given the volatile political situation and multiple military coup attempts, the President acted within lawful executive discretion in determining that the return of the Marcos family posed a grave threat to national security.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition and UPHELD the President's power to bar the return of the Marcoses.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 1 (Plenary Executive Power & Residual Powers) & Article III, Section 6 (Right to Travel Limits) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Marcos v. Manglapus is the monumental landmark establishing the Doctrine of Residual Powers of the President: Executive Power under Article VII, Section 1 is an expansive grant of sovereign power extending beyond enumerated textual authorities.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 99

Ocampo vs. Rear Admiral Enriquez

CITATION 807 SCRA 727, G.R. No. 225973, November 8, 2016 | Supreme Court En Banc (Peralta, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

President Rodrigo Duterte issued a verbal directive to Defense Secretary Delfin Lorenzana to implement his campaign promise allowing the burial of the remains of former President Ferdinand E. Marcos at the Libingan ng mga Bayani (LNMB). Saturnino Ocampo, human rights victims of Martial Law, and others filed petitions for certiorari and prohibition to stop the burial, arguing that Marcos was a dictator convicted of human rights violations and disqualified from heroic burial.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether President Duterte committed grave abuse of discretion under his executive power of control in allowing the burial of former President Marcos at the LNMB.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. President Duterte acted within constitutional executive discretion under his power of control over public military property.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 1 and Section 17 of the 1987 Constitution ('The President shall have control of all executive departments, bureaus, and offices'); AFP Regulations G 161-375.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that the Libingan ng mga Bayani is a national military cemetery under the administrative control of the Department of National Defense and the AFP, which are executive agencies under the direct constitutional control of the President under Article VII, Section 17. Under AFP Regulations G 161-375, former Presidents and Commanders-in-Chief are eligible for interment at LNMB unless dishonorably separated or convicted of crimes involving moral turpitude by a court-martial. Marcos was never dishonorably discharged by the military. The President's decision to permit the burial was an exercise of executive policy and discretion within constitutional bounds.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petitions and PERMITTED the burial of former President Marcos at the Libingan ng mga Bayani.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 17 (Presidential Power of Executive Control) & Article VII, Section 1 (Executive Policy Discretion) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Ocampo v. Enriquez illustrates the scope of the Presidential Power of Executive Control over public lands and military cemeteries, confirming that executive policy decisions within statutory rules are shielded from judicial overturning.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 100

Power Sector Assets and Liabilities Management Corp. (PSALM) vs. Commissioner of Internal Revenue

CITATION 835 SCRA 235, G.R. No. 198146, August 8, 2017 | Supreme Court En Banc (Carpio, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

PSALM, a government-owned corporation, sold generation assets (Pantabangan-Masiway and Magat power plants) pursuant to the EPIRA law. The Bureau of Internal Revenue (BIR) assessed PSALM with P3.81 billion in Value-Added Tax (VAT). PSALM filed a petition for dispute resolution before the Department of Justice (DOJ) pursuant to Presidential Decree No. 242 (Administrative Settlement of Disputes between Government Agencies). The BIR argued that tax disputes fall under the exclusive jurisdiction of the Court of Tax Appeals (CTA), not the DOJ.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether disputes between wholly-owned government agencies and corporations regarding taxes must be administratively settled by the Secretary of Justice / President under PD 242.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. Under Presidential Decree No. 242 and the President's constitutional power of control, inter-agency government disputes MUST be administratively resolved within the Executive.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 17 of the 1987 Constitution ('The President shall have control of all the executive departments, bureaus, and offices. He shall ensure that the laws be faithfully executed'); Presidential Decree No. 242; Sections 66-71, Chapter 14, Book IV, Administrative Code of 1987.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that the President's constitutional power of control under Article VII, Section 17 means the power to alter, modify, nullify, or set aside the acts of subordinate executive officials and substitute the President's own judgment. PD 242 establishes a mandatory administrative mechanism for the settlement of disputes between government-owned corporations and line bureaus (like BIR and PSALM) before the Secretary of Justice (alter ego of the President). It is unseemly and wasteful for one executive agency to sue another in court. The CTA has no jurisdiction over intra-governmental disputes that are subject to mandatory presidential administrative settlement.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED PSALM's petition and UPHELD the exclusive jurisdiction of the DOJ/Executive to resolve inter-agency tax disputes.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 17 (Presidential Power of Control & Faithfully Executed Clause) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: PSALM v. CIR establishes that the President's constitutional power of control over the Executive mandates the internal administrative settlement of disputes between government agencies under PD 242, barring intra-executive litigation.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 101

Ganzon vs. Court of Appeals

CITATION 200 SCRA 271, G.R. Nos. 93252, 93746 & 95245, August 5, 1991 | Supreme Court En Banc (Sarmiento, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Rodolfo Ganzon, the elected Mayor of Iloilo City, was subjected to a series of administrative complaints for grave misconduct and abuse of authority filed before the Department of Local Government (DLG). DLG Secretary Luis Santos issued successive preventive suspension orders against Mayor Ganzon totaling over 180 days, effectively barring him from office for the duration of his term. Ganzon challenged the suspension orders before the courts.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the President's constitutional power of general supervision over local government units under Article X, Section 4 includes the power of executive control.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. The President exercises only GENERAL SUPERVISION, NOT CONTROL, over local governments; excessive successive suspensions constitute an unlawful exercise of control.

LEGAL BASIS Article X, Section 4 of the 1987 Constitution ('The President of the Philippines shall exercise general supervision over local governments'); Article VII, Section 17 (Power of Control over Executive Line Bureaus); Doctrine of Local Autonomy.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc formulated the classic distinction between Control and Supervision: 'Control' is the power to direct, alter, modify, or substitute one's own discretion for that of the subordinate. 'Supervision' is merely overseeing to ensure that subordinate officers perform their duties in accordance with law; it does not include the power to substitute judgment. The President and the DLG Secretary possess only general supervision over local government units. While preventive suspension may be issued under strict statutory grounds, imposing repeated, successive 60-day suspensions without trial paralyzes local elective governance, crossing the line from lawful supervision into unconstitutional control.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court LIFTED the successive preventive suspensions and ORDERED Mayor Ganzon reinstated to his elective post.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article X, Section 4 (Presidential General Supervision over LGUs) vs. Article VII, Section 17 (Power of Control) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Ganzon v. CA is the seminal case delineating Presidential Supervision from Control: the President cannot exercise control over local government units, protecting constitutional local autonomy.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 102

Lacson vs. Romero

CITATION 84 Phil. 740, G.R. No. L-3081, October 14, 1949 | Supreme Court (Montemayor, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Arsenio H. Lacson was duly appointed Provincial Fiscal of Negros Oriental, confirmed by the Commission on Appointments, and assumed office. Subsequently, the President nominated Lacson to the post of Provincial Fiscal of Tarlac, which the Commission on Appointments also confirmed. Lacson adamantly refused to accept the appointment to Tarlac and continued discharging his functions in Negros Oriental. The President then appointed respondent Honesto Romero as Provincial Fiscal of Negros Oriental. Lacson filed a petition for quo warranto.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

What are the essential stages of a valid public appointment, and can an appointee be transferred or removed from office without his consent (Acceptance).

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER Appointment requires FOUR STAGES (Nomination, Confirmation, Issuance of Commission, and Acceptance); an appointment is incomplete without Acceptance.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 16 of the Constitution; Civil Service Security of Tenure (Article IX-B, Section 2[3]); Doctrine of Stages of Appointment.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the appointment process consists of four distinct, indispensable phases: (1) Nomination by the President, (2) Confirmation by the Commission on Appointments, (3) Issuance of the Commission, and (4) Acceptance by the appointee. Acceptance is indispensable to complete the appointment because no citizen can be coerced into accepting a public office against his will. Because Lacson rejected the appointment to Tarlac, that appointment never took effect, and his original tenure as Fiscal of Negros Oriental remained intact. Transferring him without his consent constituted an unlawful removal violating civil service security of tenure.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED Lacson's petition and declared Romero's appointment NULL and VOID.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 16 (Appointing Power Stages) & Article IX-B, Section 2(3) (Civil Service Security of Tenure) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Lacson v. Romero establishes the Four Stages of Public Appointment and the rule that acceptance is essential to complete an appointment, safeguarding security of tenure against unconsented lateral transfers.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 103

Aguinaldo vs. President Benigno Aquino III

CITATION 811 SCRA 301, G.R. No. 224302, November 29, 2016 | Supreme Court En Banc (Leonardo-De Castro, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

President Benigno Aquino III was presented with six (6) separate shortlists prepared by the Judicial and Bar Council (JBC) for six (6) simultaneous vacancies in the Sandiganbayan (16th to 21st Associate Justices). Instead of choosing one appointee from each specific shortlist, President Aquino cross-picked nominees across different lists (clustering). Petitioners challenged the appointments, arguing that clustering infringed upon the constitutional nominating power of the JBC.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the President may cross-pick or cluster nominees across separate JBC shortlists for multiple judicial vacancies.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. The President is constitutionally bound to select ONE appointee from EACH specific shortlist submitted by the JBC for each distinct judicial vacancy.

LEGAL BASIS Article VIII, Section 9 of the 1987 Constitution ('The Members of the Supreme Court and judges of lower courts shall be appointed by the President from a list of at least three nominees prepared by the Judicial and Bar Council for every vacancy'); Article VII, Section 16.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that Article VIII, Section 9 textually requires the President to appoint 'from a list of at least three nominees prepared by the JBC for every vacancy.' Each judicial vacancy is independent and must correspond to a separate shortlist. When the JBC submits a shortlist for a specific vacancy, the President's appointing power is limited to choosing one name from that specific list. The President cannot merge, cluster, or cross-pick names between different shortlists because doing so circumvents the JBC's constitutional vetting prerogative and distorts the shortlist requirements.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court HELD that clustering shortlists is UNCONSTITUTIONAL, but sustained the de facto status of the sitting justices.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VIII, Section 9 (Judicial Appointments from JBC Shortlists for Every Vacancy) & Article VII, Section 16 Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Aguinaldo v. Aquino III establishes the 'One Shortlist Per Vacancy Rule': the President cannot cluster or pool separate JBC shortlists for judicial appointments.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 104

In Re: Appointment of Valenzuela and Vallarta

CITATION 298 SCRA 408, A.M. No. 98-5-01-SC, November 9, 1998 | Supreme Court En Banc (Narvasa, C.J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

On March 30, 1998 (during the constitutional election ban period preceding the May 11, 1998 presidential elections), President Fidel V. Ramos signed the appointments of Mateo Valenzuela and Placido Vallarta as Regional Trial Court judges. The Supreme Court En Banc motu proprio examined the validity of the appointments in light of the constitutional ban on 'midnight appointments' under Article VII, Section 15.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the constitutional ban on midnight appointments under Article VII, Section 15 applies to appointments in the Judiciary.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The prohibition against midnight appointments under Article VII, Section 15 applies to all appointments in the Judiciary.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 15 of the 1987 Constitution ('Two months immediately before the next presidential elections and up to the end of his term, a President or Acting President shall not make appointments, except temporary appointments to executive positions when continued vacancies therein will prejudice public service or endanger public safety').

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that Article VII, Section 15 is a comprehensive prohibition designed to prevent an outgoing President from stacking the civil service or the judiciary with partisan appointees (midnight appointments) to influence the incoming administration. The text provides only one single exception: temporary appointments to executive positions to prevent prejudice to public service or public safety. Because judicial appointments are permanent and non-executive, they fall squarely within the general ban. Consequently, the President cannot appoint judges during the two-month period immediately preceding a presidential election.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DECLARED the judicial appointments of Valenzuela and Vallarta NULL and VOID.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 15 (Constitutional Ban on Midnight Appointments) & Article VIII, Section 9 (Judicial Appointments) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Valenzuela & Vallarta is the seminal ruling applying the Midnight Appointments Ban to lower court judicial appointments, protecting the integrity of the judicial branch during presidential transitions.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 105

Atty. Velicaria-Garafil vs. Office of the President

CITATION 760 SCRA 323, G.R. No. 203372, June 16, 2015 | Supreme Court En Banc (Carpio, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

President Gloria Macapagal-Arroyo issued hundreds of appointment papers in March 2010 immediately prior to the start of the midnight appointment ban. Upon assuming office, President Benigno Aquino III issued Executive Order No. 2 revoking and declaring void all appointments made by President Arroyo on or after March 11, 2010, that failed to comply with the constitutional requirements of transmittal and acceptance. Atty. Cheloy Velicaria-Garafil and other affected appointees challenged the validity of EO 2.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

What are the mandatory elements to exempt an appointment from the constitutional ban on midnight appointments under Article VII, Section 15.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER To be valid and exempt from the ban, all four elements of the appointment process (Signing, Transmittal, Receipt, and Acceptance) must occur BEFORE the ban starts.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 15 of the 1987 Constitution; Four Requisites of a Completed Public Appointment.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc laid down the definitive 'Four Requisites Rule' for appointment validity during transition periods: (1) The appointment paper must be SIGNED by the President before March 11; (2) The appointment paper must be OFFICIALLY TRANSMITTED to the appointee before March 11; (3) The appointment must be RECEIVED by the appointee before March 11; and (4) The appointee must ACCEPT the appointment (by taking the oath) before March 11. An appointment is not a unilateral secret act; until the appointment paper leaves the President's physical control and is officially transmitted and accepted, it remains incomplete. Backdated appointments transmitted during the ban are void midnight appointments. EO 2 was a valid exercise of presidential revocation.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court UPHELD the constitutionality of Executive Order No. 2 and DISMISSED the petitions.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 15 (The Four Requisites of Non-Midnight Appointments) & Article VII, Section 16 Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Velicaria-Garafil establishes the Four Requisites Rule for public appointments, ensuring that outgoing executives cannot defeat the Midnight Appointments Ban through backdated or unreleased appointment papers.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 106

De Castro vs. Judicial and Bar Council (JBC)

CITATION 615 SCRA 666, G.R. No. 191002, April 20, 2010 | Supreme Court En Banc (Bersamin, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Chief Justice Reynato S. Puno was scheduled to retire compulsorily on May 17, 2010 (seven days after the May 10, 2010 presidential elections). The JBC began compiling nominations for the next Chief Justice. Petitioners Arturo De Castro and others sought a ruling that President Gloria Macapagal-Arroyo had the constitutional duty to appoint the next Chief Justice, arguing that the midnight appointments ban in Article VII, Section 15 does not apply to vacancies in the Supreme Court.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the constitutional prohibition on midnight appointments under Article VII, Section 15 applies to appointments to the Supreme Court under Article VIII, Section 4(1).

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. The midnight appointments ban under Article VII, Section 15 does NOT apply to appointments to the Supreme Court.

LEGAL BASIS Article VIII, Section 4(1) of the 1987 Constitution ('Any vacancy shall be filled within ninety days from the occurrence thereof'); Article VII, Section 15 (Midnight Appointments Ban); Statutory Construction (General vs Special Provision).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that Article VII, Section 15 is located in Article VII governing the Executive Department, whereas Article VIII, Section 4(1) is located in Article VIII governing the Judicial Department. Article VIII, Section 4(1) contains a mandatory constitutional directive to the President: any vacancy in the Supreme Court 'SHALL be filled within ninety days from the occurrence thereof.' This 90-day period is strict and contains no exceptions for election periods. Had the framers intended the midnight appointments ban to apply to the Supreme Court, they would have explicitly subordinated Article VIII, Section 4(1) to Article VII, Section 15. Therefore, the Valenzuela doctrine was partially modified as to the Supreme Court.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court HELD that the President may lawfully appoint the Chief Justice and Supreme Court Justices during the election ban period.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VIII, Section 4(1) (Mandatory 90-Day Period to Fill SC Vacancies) vs. Article VII, Section 15 (Midnight Appointments Ban) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: De Castro v. JBC creates the landmark 'Supreme Court Exception' to the Midnight Appointments Ban: the President is constitutionally mandated to fill vacancies in the Supreme Court within 90 days regardless of the election ban period.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 107

De Rama vs. Court of Appeals

CITATION 353 SCRA 94, G.R. No. 131136, February 28, 2001 | Supreme Court En Banc (Ynares-Santiago, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Outgoing Mayor Conrado de Rama of Pagbilao, Quezon appointed 14 municipal employees in May 1995 prior to the commencement of the election appointment ban. The appointments were approved by the Civil Service Commission (CSC). When newly elected Mayor Venancio Mereno assumed office, he summarily revoked the appointments, claiming they were midnight appointments.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether appointments made by an outgoing local chief executive prior to the statutory election ban period constitute illegal midnight appointments.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Appointments made prior to the commencement of the ban period and strictly complying with civil service qualifications are VALID and cannot be arbitrarily revoked.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 15 of the Constitution; Civil Service Law (PD 807 / EO 292); Article IX-B, Section 2(3) (Security of Tenure).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court held that the constitutional and statutory ban on midnight appointments is pegged to specific time periods. Appointments made before the start of the prohibited period by an outgoing executive are presumed regular, valid, and lawful if the appointees possess the required civil service eligibility and qualifications. The mere fact that an appointment was made near the end of an official's term does not make it a 'midnight appointment.' Once an appointment is perfected and approved by the CSC, the appointee acquires security of tenure and cannot be summarily dismissed without cause.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court REVERSED the revocation orders and ORDERED the reinstatement of the 14 municipal employees.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 15 (Midnight Appointment Bounds) & Article IX-B, Section 2(3) (Civil Service Tenure) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: De Rama v. CA clarifies that the Midnight Appointments Ban is not a subjective label; appointments executed before the statutory ban starts are valid and protected by Civil Service security of tenure.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 108

Funa vs. Chairman, Commission on Audit

CITATION 670 SCRA 579, G.R. No. 192791, April 24, 2012 | Supreme Court En Banc (Leonardo-De Castro, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Reynaldo A. Villar was appointed COA Commissioner in 2004 for a 7-year term to expire in 2011. In 2008, following the resignation of Chairman Guillermo Carague, President Gloria Macapagal-Arroyo appointed Villar as COA Chairman for a fresh 7-year term to expire in 2015. Atty. Dennis Funa challenged the appointment, arguing that promoting a sitting commissioner to chairman for a full new term violated the constitutional term rotation and ban on reappointment under Article IX-D, Section 1(2).

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether an incumbent Commissioner of a Constitutional Commission can be appointed as Chairman for a fresh 7-year term.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. An incumbent Commissioner cannot be appointed Chairman for a fresh 7-year term; the appointment violates the rotational term system and the ban on reappointment.

LEGAL BASIS Article IX-D, Section 1(2) of the 1987 Constitution ('The Chairman and the Commissioners shall be appointed by the President with the consent of the Commission on Appointments for a term of seven years without reappointment'); Nationalista Party v. De Vera.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that the Constitution established a strict 7-5-3 year staggered term system for Constitutional Commissions (COA, COMELEC, CSC) to ensure continuity and independence, with one vacancy occurring every two years. To permit an incumbent Commissioner to be appointed Chairman for a fresh 7-year term would: (1) allow the appointee to serve for more than the 7-year constitutional maximum, (2) violate the express ban on 'reappointment', and (3) destroy the staggered 2-year rotational cycle. A sitting commissioner may only be promoted to Chairman if the remaining unexpired portion of the term does not exceed the 7-year aggregate cap.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DECLARED the appointment of Villar as COA Chairman for a fresh 7-year term UNCONSTITUTIONAL and VOID.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article IX-D, Section 1(2) (COA 7-Year Term, Staggered Cycle & Non-Reappointment Rule) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Funa v. COA Chairman establishes the strict constitutional rule safeguarding the Staggered Rotational System of Constitutional Commissions: no commissioner may serve beyond the 7-year maximum through promotional reappointment.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 109

Kulayan vs. Tan

CITATION 675 SCRA 482, G.R. No. 187298, July 3, 2012 | Supreme Court En Banc (Sereno, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following the kidnapping of three International Committee of the Red Cross (ICRC) workers by the Abu Sayyaf in Sulu, Sulu Provincial Governor Abdusakur Tan issued Proclamation 1-09 declaring a 'State of Emergency in the Province of Sulu,' set up military checkpoints, ordered warrantless searches, and called out the Armed Forces of the Philippines (AFP) and PNP. Petitioners challenged the Governor's proclamation, asserting that calling out the armed forces is an exclusive presidential power.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether a provincial governor has the constitutional or statutory authority to declare a state of emergency and exercise the Calling-Out power over the military.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. The Calling-Out power over the Armed Forces belongs SOLELY and EXCLUSIVELY to the President of the Philippines as Commander-in-Chief.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 18 of the 1987 Constitution ('The President shall be the Commander-in-Chief of all armed forces of the Philippines and whenever it becomes necessary, he may call out such armed forces to prevent or suppress lawless violence, invasion or rebellion'); Local Government Code (RA 7160).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that the Commander-in-Chief powers (including the calling-out power, martial law, and suspension of the writ) are non-delegable constitutional prerogatives exclusively vested in the President. Provincial governors and local chief executives possess administrative powers under the Local Government Code over local police, but they have no authority whatsoever to declare a state of emergency, order military deployments, or call out the armed forces. Governor Tan's proclamation was a clear usurpation of the President's exclusive constitutional authority under Article VII, Section 18.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition and declared Proclamation 1-09 and its implementing guidelines UNCONSTITUTIONAL and VOID.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 18 (Exclusive Presidential Calling-Out Power) & Article X (Limits of Local Government Powers) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Kulayan v. Tan establishes that the Calling-Out Power over the military is indivisible and exclusive to the President of the Philippines; local governors cannot declare states of emergency or command armed forces units.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 110

Integrated Bar of the Philippines vs. Zamora

CITATION 338 SCRA 81, G.R. No. 141284, August 15, 2000 | Supreme Court En Banc (Kapunan, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

President Joseph Estrada directed the Armed Forces of the Philippines (AFP) to assist the Philippine National Police (PNP) in conducting joint visibility patrols in Metro Manila to curb a wave of violent crime, kidnappings, and bank robberies. The Integrated Bar of the Philippines (IBP) filed a petition for certiorari and prohibition, arguing that deploying Philippine Marines for civilian law enforcement violated civilian supremacy and equal protection.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the President's calling out of the Armed Forces to assist the police in civilian visibility patrols is subject to judicial review and constitutionally valid.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES, it is subject to judicial review for grave abuse of discretion, and YES, the calling out was CONSTITUTIONALLY VALID.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 18 of the 1987 Constitution ('The President shall be the Commander-in-Chief... and whenever it becomes necessary, he may call out such armed forces to prevent or suppress lawless violence'); Article II, Section 3 (Civilian Supremacy).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that the Calling-Out Power is the least restrictive of the President's three Commander-in-Chief powers (Calling Out, Suspension of the Writ, Martial Law). The Court has the power of judicial review under the expanded certiorari jurisdiction to determine if the President committed grave abuse of discretion. Here, the Marines were deployed merely in support of the police, under police operational supervision, unarmed with heavy combat weapons, and without civilian courts being closed. The President acted within broad executive discretion to prevent lawless violence without violating civilian supremacy.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the IBP petition and UPHELD the validity of the joint PNP-Marine visibility patrols.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 18 (The Calling-Out Power) & Article II, Section 3 (Civilian Supremacy Preservation) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: IBP v. Zamora establishes the constitutional framework for the Calling-Out Power: the President possesses wide factual discretion to call out the military to support police operations, subject to judicial review for grave abuse of discretion.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 111

Padilla vs. Congress of the Philippines

CITATION 832 SCRA 282, G.R. No. 231671, July 25, 2017 | Supreme Court En Banc (Leonardo-De Castro, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following President Duterte's issuance of Proclamation No. 216 declaring martial law in Mindanao, the Senate and House of Representatives separately passed resolutions supporting the proclamation without convening in a joint session. Petitioners, led by Michael Trevor Padilla and former Senator Wigberto Tañada, filed a petition for mandamus to compel Congress to convene in joint session to vote on the martial law proclamation.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether Congress is constitutionally mandated under Article VII, Section 18 to convene in a joint session when the President proclaims martial law, even if Congress does not intend to revoke it.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Congress is required to convene in joint session and vote jointly ONLY IF it intends to REVOKE or EXTEND martial law.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 18, paragraph 1 of the 1987 Constitution ('The Congress, voting jointly, by a vote of at least a majority of all its Members in regular or special session, may revoke such proclamation or suspension, which revocation shall not be set aside by the President'); Fortun v. Macapagal-Arroyo.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that under the plain text of Article VII, Section 18, par. 1, a joint session of Congress is mandatory only when Congress exercises its power to REVOKE the proclamation or when the President requests an EXTENSION. The Constitution does not require congressional approval for martial law to take effect; it takes effect immediately upon presidential proclamation. If both Houses are in agreement and support the President's action, convening in joint session to vote would be an idle ceremony. Mandamus will not lie to compel Congress to convene jointly when there is no intention to revoke.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED the petition for Mandamus for lack of merit.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 18, par. 1 (Congressional Joint Session Mechanics for Martial Law Revocation/Extension) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Padilla v. Congress clarifies the operational rules of Article VII, Section 18: Congress is not required to convene in joint session unless it seeks to revoke martial law or vote on a requested extension.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 112

Olaguer vs. Military Commission No. 34

CITATION 150 SCRA 144, G.R. No. L-54558, May 22, 1987 | Supreme Court En Banc (Gancayco, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Eduardo Olaguer and other civilians were charged before Military Commission No. 34 with subversion and terrorism during Martial Law. The military commission convicted Olaguer and sentenced him to death by electrocution. Olaguer challenged the jurisdiction of military tribunals to try civilians when civil courts were open and functioning, seeking to overturn the Marcos-era Aquino v. Military Commission doctrine.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether military commissions have jurisdiction to try and convict civilians for criminal offenses during martial law when civil courts are open and functioning (The Open Court Doctrine).

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. Military commissions have NO JURISDICTION over civilians as long as civilian courts are open and functioning.

LEGAL BASIS The Open Court Doctrine; Article VII, Section 18, paragraph 4 of the 1987 Constitution ('A state of martial law does not suspend the operation of the Constitution, nor supplant the functioning of the civil courts or legislative assemblies, nor authorize the conferment of jurisdiction on military courts and agencies over civilians where civil courts are able to function'); Article III, Section 1 (Due Process).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc explicitly OVERTURNED the prior ruling in Aquino v. Military Commission (63 SCRA 546). The Court adopted the 'Open Court Doctrine': military tribunals pertain to the military establishment and are not part of the judicial system. Due process guarantees that every civilian is entitled to trial by regular, independent civilian courts with constitutional safeguards. As long as regular civil courts are open and operating, subjecting a civilian to military tribunal jurisdiction is a nullity and unconstitutional. All military commission judgments against civilians were declared void ab initio.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, VACATED the death sentence, and declared all military commission proceedings against civilians NULL and VOID.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 18, par. 4 (The Open Court Doctrine) & Article III, Section 14 (Due Process in Criminal Proceedings) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Olaguer v. Military Commission is the historic landmark institutionalizing the Open Court Doctrine in Philippine law, now expressly codified in Article VII, Section 18, par. 4 of the 1987 Constitution.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 113

Macapagal-Arroyo vs. People

CITATION 797 SCRA 241, G.R. No. 220598, July 19, 2016 | Supreme Court En Banc (Bersamin, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Former President Gloria Macapagal-Arroyo was charged with Plunder before the Sandiganbayan involving the alleged misuse of P366 million in Philippine Charity Sweepstakes Office (PCSO) confidential intelligence funds. The Sandiganbayan denied her Demurrer to Evidence, applying the Doctrine of Command Responsibility to hold her liable based on her written approval of PCSO budget requests. Arroyo filed a petition for certiorari before the Supreme Court.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the Doctrine of Command Responsibility can be applied in domestic criminal plunder prosecutions to substitute for proof of overt conspiratorial acts.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. The Doctrine of Command Responsibility applies in international humanitarian law / armed conflict, NOT as a substitute for proof of criminal conspiracy in domestic plunder trials.

LEGAL BASIS Republic Act No. 7080 (The Plunder Law); Article III, Section 14(2) (Presumption of Innocence); Doctrine of Command Responsibility Limits; Principle of Personal Criminal Culpability.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that in criminal prosecutions for Plunder under RA 7080, the prosecution must prove beyond reasonable doubt: (1) the predicate unlawful acts of amassing ill-gotten wealth, (2) the identification of the 'main plunderer' who benefited, and (3) specific overt acts demonstrating conspiracy. The prosecution failed to show that Arroyo received or pocketed any PCSO funds. The Sandiganbayan gravely abused its discretion in applying 'Command Responsibility' - a doctrine developed for war crimes and military command negligence - to create vicarious criminal liability for plunder. Mere approval of budget requests does not constitute overt criminal conspiracy.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, REVERSED the Sandiganbayan orders, GRANTED Arroyo's Demurrer to Evidence, and ORDERED her immediate release from detention.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article III, Section 14(2) (Presumption of Innocence) & Article VII, Section 1 (Executive Powers & Non-Applicability of Command Responsibility to Domestic Crimes) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Macapagal-Arroyo v. People defines the boundaries of criminal liability for executive officials: the military doctrine of Command Responsibility cannot be used to bypass proof of overt conspiratorial acts in domestic anti-graft prosecutions.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 114

Monsanto vs. Factoran, Jr.

CITATION 170 SCRA 190, G.R. No. 78239, February 9, 1989 | Supreme Court En Banc (Cortés, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Salvacion A. Monsanto, Assistant City Treasurer of Calbayog City, was convicted of Estafa through Falsification of Public Documents by the Sandiganbayan. While her appeal was pending, President Ferdinand Marcos granted her an 'absolute and unconditional pardon.' Monsanto demanded to be automatically reinstated to her former position in the Bureau of Forest Development (DENR) without fresh appointment and awarded full back salaries. DENR Secretary Fulgencio Factoran rejected her claim.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether an absolute pardon automatically obliterates civil guilt, restores an employee to public office, and entitles the pardonee to back salaries.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER NO. An absolute pardon exempts the individual from punishment, but does NOT erase moral guilt or automatically reinstate the pardonee to public office without a fresh appointment.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 19 of the 1987 Constitution ('Except in cases of impeachment, or as otherwise provided in this Constitution, the President may grant reprieves, commutations, and pardons, and remit fines and forfeitures, after conviction by final judgment'); Principles of Executive Clemency.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that pardon looks to the future (prospectively). Pardon relieves the convict of the penal consequences and restores civil and political rights (such as the right to vote and eligibility for future appointment). However, pardon does NOT erase the historical fact of the offense or the moral guilt. Public office is a public trust, not private property. Pardon does not operate as an automatic writ of reinstatement to the vacated post. To re-enter public service, the pardonee must secure a fresh appointment from the appointing authority, and is not entitled to back salaries for services never rendered.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED Monsanto's petition and UPHELD Secretary Factoran's refusal to automatically reinstate her.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 19 (Executive Clemency: Nature and Legal Effects of Pardon) & Article XI, Section 1 (Public Office is a Public Trust) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Monsanto v. Factoran is the definitive authority on the legal effects of Executive Pardon: pardon remits the criminal penalty but does not erase guilt, require automatic reinstatement, or confer back pay.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 115

Garcia vs. Chairman, Commission on Audit

CITATION 226 SCRA 199, G.R. No. 75025, September 14, 1993 | Supreme Court En Banc (Bellosillo, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Petitioner Florencio Garcia, a supervising lineman in the Bureau of Telecommunications, was summarily dismissed from service for alleged theft of government cable wires. Nine years later, the President granted Garcia 'executive clemency,' clearing him of administrative guilt. Garcia was subsequently reinstated and claimed payment of back salaries for the period he was unlawfully separated. The COA Chairman denied the back salary claim, citing the Monsanto doctrine.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether an employee reinstated pursuant to executive clemency based on a finding of innocence is entitled to back salaries.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. When executive clemency is grounded on INNOCENCE or wrongful dismissal, the employee is entitled to reinstatement WITH back salaries.

LEGAL BASIS Article VII, Section 19 of the Constitution; Sabello v. DECS (180 SCRA 623); Exception to the Monsanto Doctrine.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc distinguished Garcia from Monsanto. In Monsanto, the employee was judicially convicted of estafa and granted pardon as an act of executive grace, which does not erase guilt. In Garcia, the executive clemency was explicitly granted because an administrative review proved that the employee was completely innocent and falsely accused. Where executive clemency is founded upon the complete innocence of the employee, justice and equity demand that the employee be restored to his full status quo ante, including the payment of back salaries for the duration of his unjust separation.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition, REVERSED the COA decision, and ORDERED the payment of back salaries to Garcia.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 19 (Executive Clemency Grounded on Innocence) & Article IX-B (Civil Service Equity) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Garcia v. Chairman establishes the 'Innocence Exception' to the Monsanto rule: executive clemency based on clear innocence entitles the wrongfully separated employee to full back wages and complete restoration.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 116

In Re: Wilfredo Sumulong Torres

CITATION 251 SCRA 709, G.R. No. 122338, December 29, 1995 | Supreme Court En Banc (Hermosisima, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Wilfredo Sumulong Torres was convicted of Estafa and sentenced to imprisonment. In 1979, the President granted Torres a 'conditional pardon' on the condition that he 'shall not again violate any of the penal laws of the Philippines.' In 1986, while on conditional pardon, Torres was charged with multiple fresh counts of estafa in various trial courts. President Corazon Aquino, acting under Section 64(i) of the Revised Administrative Code, ordered the immediate arrest and recommitment of Torres to serve the unexpired portion of his sentence.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the President may determine the breach of a conditional pardon and order the recommitment of a convict without prior judicial conviction.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The President possesses the statutory authority under Section 64(i) of the Revised Administrative Code to determine breach of conditional pardon and order arrest.

LEGAL BASIS Section 64(i) of the Revised Administrative Code of 1917; Article VII, Section 19 of the Constitution; Sales v. Director of Prisons (87 Phil. 492).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that a conditional pardon is a contract between the Chief Executive and the convict: the convict agrees to strictly observe the conditions in exchange for liberty. Under Section 64(i) of the Revised Administrative Code, the President is empowered to determine administratively whether the conditions of the pardon have been violated, and to order the rearrest and recommitment of the convict without need for a prior judicial conviction. While Article 159 of the Revised Penal Code provides a judicial remedy for violation of conditional pardon, Section 64(i) provides a concurrent, independent executive remedy.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DENIED Torres' petition for habeas corpus and SUSTAINED his lawful recommitment to prison.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VII, Section 19 (Executive Clemency & Conditional Pardon Enforcement) & Section 64(i) Revised Administrative Code Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: In Re Torres confirms the President's administrative authority to enforce conditional pardons: the Chief Executive can order the immediate rearrest and re-incarceration of a convict who breaches clemency terms.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 117

Philippine Constitution Association (PHILCONSA) vs. Enriquez

CITATION 235 SCRA 506, G.R. No. 113105, August 19, 1994 | Supreme Court En Banc (Quiason, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

President Fidel V. Ramos vetoed several special provisions in the 1994 General Appropriations Act (GAA / RA 7663) and issued conditions on fund releases. PHILCONSA and several members of Congress challenged the President's vetoes, as well as the executive practice of 'impoundment' (the executive refusal to spend appropriated funds).

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

1. Whether the President has the constitutional power of Executive Impoundment to withhold or refuse to spend appropriated funds. 2. Whether the presidential item-veto of special provisions was constitutional.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER 1. Impoundment is permissible ONLY when authorized by express statutory delegation or guidelines in the appropriations law. 2. YES. The item-veto was valid where the provisions were unconstitutional riders or inappropriate provisions.

LEGAL BASIS Article VI, Section 27(2) of the 1987 Constitution ('The President shall have the power to veto any particular item or items in an appropriation, revenue, or tariff bill'); Article VI, Section 29(1); Principles of Executive Impoundment.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that 'impoundment' refers to the refusal of the President to spend funds appropriated by Congress. Appropriations laws are generally authorizations, not mandatory commands, to spend. However, the President cannot arbitrarily refuse to execute legislative programs.
