Chief Justice Maria Lourdes Sereno invoked Rule 10, Section 2 of the JBC Rules (unanimity rule on integrity challenges), accusing Jardeleza of taking a compromised stance in the South China Sea arbitration case against China. The JBC excluded Jardeleza from the shortlist submitted to President Aquino without giving him a formal hearing. Jardeleza petitioned for certiorari and mandamus.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the JBC violated Jardeleza's right to procedural due process when it invoked Rule 10, Section 2 to exclude him from the shortlist without formal notice and hearing.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The JBC violated Jardeleza's constitutional right to Procedural Due Process; when an applicant's integrity is challenged, specific notice and opportunity to explain are mandatory.

LEGAL BASIS Article VIII, Section 8(1) & (5) of the Constitution (Supreme Court Supervisory Power over JBC); Article III, Section 1 (Procedural Due Process); Rule 10, Section 2 of JBC-009.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that while the JBC possesses discretion in vetting candidates, that discretion is subject to the supervisory power of the Supreme Court and the Bill of Rights. Procedural due process applies to JBC proceedings. When a candidate's integrity is challenged under the special unanimity rule of Rule 10, Section 2, the candidate is constitutionally entitled to: (1) be informed in writing of the specific charges, (2) inspect the evidence against him, and (3) be given reasonable opportunity to cross-examine and defend himself. Because Jardeleza was excluded without due process, his disqualification was void.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court GRANTED the petition and DIRECTED the JBC to include Francis H. Jardeleza in the shortlist submitted to the President.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VIII, Section 8(1) (Supreme Court Supervision over JBC) & Article III, Section 1 (Due Process in JBC Nominations) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Jardeleza v. Sereno establishes that JBC shortlist vetting is subordinate to Procedural Due Process and Supreme Court supervisory review: integrity objections require fair notice and opportunity to defend.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 148

Umali vs. Judicial and Bar Council (JBC)

CITATION 832 SCRA 194, G.R. No. 228628, July 25, 2017 | Supreme Court En Banc (Velasco, Jr., J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

Following the Chavez v. JBC ruling limiting Congress to one vote, Congress adopted an internal arrangement where the Senate and House of Representatives designated two representatives to sit alternately, each possessing a 'half-vote' (0.5 vote each) in the JBC. Representative Reynaldo Umali challenged this arrangement, demanding that the Senate and House each be granted a full 1-vote representation.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the half-vote (0.5 vote) sharing arrangement between the Senate and the House of Representatives in the JBC is constitutionally valid under the Chavez doctrine.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The half-vote arrangement is CONSTITUTIONAL because it preserves the singular 1-vote allocation for Congress mandated by Article VIII, Section 8(1).

LEGAL BASIS Article VIII, Section 8(1) of the 1987 Constitution; Chavez v. JBC (676 SCRA 579); Internal Autonomy of Congress.

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc reaffirmed the Chavez ruling that Congress is entitled to only ONE collective seat and ONE vote in the JBC. The half-vote system (0.5 vote for the Senate representative and 0.5 vote for the House representative) does not increase the total voting power of Congress beyond the single vote mandated by the Constitution. It is a pragmatic, internal consensual accommodation devised by the two Houses of Congress to participate equally in the JBC without distorting the 7-member balance. The petition seeking two full votes was dismissed.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED Umali's petition and UPHELD the constitutionality of the half-vote arrangement.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VIII, Section 8(1) (Preservation of 1-Vote Congressional Representation in JBC) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Umali v. JBC reaffirms the Chavez doctrine and validates the pragmatic half-vote (0.5 vote) consensus between the Senate and the House in the Judicial and Bar Council.

――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――――

CASE 149

Judge Villanueva vs. Judicial and Bar Council (JBC)

CITATION 755 SCRA 182, G.R. No. 211833, April 7, 2015 | Supreme Court En Banc (Reyes, J.)

§ I. FACTS OF THE CASE:

The Judicial and Bar Council (JBC) adopted a policy requiring at least five (5) years of service as a first-level court judge (MTC/MTCC/MCTC) before being eligible for nomination to a second-level court (Regional Trial Court). Judge Ferdinand R. Villanueva, an MTC judge with over 4 years of service, applied for an RTC vacancy. The JBC excluded him for failing to meet the 5-year policy. Villanueva filed a petition for certiorari and prohibition, arguing that the JBC policy unconstitutionally added a qualification beyond what is prescribed in Article VIII, Section 7 of the Constitution.

II. STATEMENT OF THE CONSTITUTIONAL ISSUE:

Whether the JBC's 5-year service requirement for first-level judges applying to the RTC is a valid exercise of rule-making discretion or an unconstitutional addition of qualifications.

§ III. THE COURT'S RULING (ALAC METHOD):

ANSWER YES. The 5-year service requirement is a VALID EXERCISE of the JBC's constitutional power to set standards of competence and experience.

LEGAL BASIS Article VIII, Section 8(5) of the 1987 Constitution ('The Council shall have the principal function of recommending appointees to the Judiciary. It may exercise such other functions and duties as may be assigned to it by the Supreme Court'); Article VIII, Section 7(3) (Competence, Integrity, Probity, and Independence).

APPLICATION / ANALYSIS The Supreme Court En Banc held that Article VIII, Section 7 prescribes the minimum qualifications for judges (citizenship, age, bar membership). Under Article VIII, Section 8(5), the JBC is constitutionally empowered to evaluate and select the best and most qualified nominees. Requiring five years of actual judicial experience in first-level courts before elevation to the Regional Trial Court is a reasonable, objective, and proportional standard to assess 'proven competence and probity' under Article VIII, Section 7(3). The policy does not amend constitutional qualifications; it establishes objective guidelines for the JBC's screening discretion.

CONCLUSION / DISPOSITIVE RULING The Supreme Court DISMISSED Judge Villanueva's petition and UPHELD the validity of the JBC 5-year experience policy.

§ IV. APPLICABLE CONSTITUTIONAL ARTICLES, SECTIONS & DOCTRINAL EXPLANATION:

Applicable Constitutional Provision(s): Article VIII, Section 8(5) (JBC Standard-Setting Authority) & Article VIII, Section 7(3) (Constitutional Qualifications of Competence) Doctrinal Synthesis & Syllabus Linkage: Judge Villanueva v. JBC defines the constitutional scope of the JBC's screening authority: the JBC may establish reasonable, objective experience criteria to determine candidate competence without violating constitutional qualification clauses.
